Namaskar! Excellencies, delegates from around the world, leaders from business and industry, policymakers, and my friends. A very warm welcome to all of you to India. A short while ago, Mr. Bloomberg shared his thoughts here. Reflecting on his 30-year journey, he mentioned that I am his friend. He is senior to me in age and is a highly successful person in his professional field. He is not only successful but also deeply committed. The affection he has for India is something I have experienced very closely, and therefore, on a personal level, I have always respected and admired him. Even today, his affection for India and his sentiments towards India on global platforms have consistently been a source of very positive energy. For that, sir, I express my heartfelt gratitude. Every year, this Forum brings together some of the finest minds in the world. Discussions here take place on the economy, technology, energy, and global affairs. And this time, for the very first time, the event is being held in India. I warmly congratulate Mr. Bloomberg and his entire team for this decision. You have chosen the right place at the right time. Friends, This year's Bloomberg Economic Forum is focused on the new powers emerging around the world. Every era has had its own sources of power and its own definition of power. But in the 21st century, especially in this third decade, a major transformation is taking place. Today, Technology is Power, Data is Power, Knowledge is Power, Innovation is Power. But friends, The real question is not who possesses power. Rather, the question is how that power is being used. Will technology advance only a few people? Or will it create new opportunities for millions? Will knowledge remain the strength of only a select few? Or will it enhance everyone's capabilities? It is from here that a new idea emerges. And that idea is: Power to Empower. Power to Empower. Beyond Power to Control lies Power to Enable. Beyond Power to Compete lies Power to Collaborate. Beyond Power to Succeed lies Power to Help Others Succeed. And therefore, the vision with which I have come here today is: Power to Empower. Friends, This idea is not new to India. In our tradition, it is said: "Vidya Vivadaya Dhanam Madaya, Shaktih Paresham Paripidanaya; Khalasya Sadhoh Viparitam Etat, Jnanaya Danaya Cha Rakshanaya." Its essence is that whoever possesses knowledge, wealth, or power, what matters is how they use it. In other words, the true value of power lies in its purpose. And it is very important to understand this philosophy in today's world. Friends, This human spirit of Power to Empower is not merely a lesson meant to be preached to others. It is something that our traditions have taught us over thousands of years. And in India, we have demonstrated this by incorporating it into our governance and our work culture. Therefore, I will continue my remarks through examples from India itself. Friends, As all of you know, India is the Mother of Democracy. India has more than one hundred languages, nearly eighty thousand registered newspapers and publications, and more than one thousand satellite television channels. And not only that, in this age of social media, India has more than five hundred million YouTube users and more than five hundred million Instagram users. In our country, people openly debate different issues, and citizens discuss diverse viewpoints. In the present era, one factor that has played a major role in enabling this is digital power. Friends, Until about twelve or thirteen years ago, there was a time in India when access to mobile phones and the internet itself was a form of power. Whoever had access also had information, knowledge, and opportunities. After 2014, when a new government came to power in India and we were given the opportunity to serve, India placed great emphasis on making mobile data affordable. As a result, today a student in a small town can access digital learning. A small shopkeeper in a village can reach new customers. A young person can enroll in online courses. In other words, connectivity in India has not merely connected people; it has connected opportunities as well. This, indeed, is Power to Empower. Friends, To those guests who have come from abroad, I would encourage you to experience India's digital payments ecosystem. In our country, a street vendor and a large car showroom may operate at completely different scales, but both use the same digital payments ecosystem. This is the strength of inclusive technology. Friends, Whatever capabilities India possesses, we do not believe in keeping them confined within our own borders. We continuously share them with the Global South and with our partner countries. We are sharing our experience in Digital Public Infrastructure with other nations so that they can develop digital systems according to their own needs. During the COVID period, we also developed the CoWIN platform, which was open source. In other words, our constant effort has been to ensure that India's capabilities open new doors of possibility for other countries as well. In other words, our strength should empower humanity. Friends, The power of innovation works in the same way. Transforming an idea into a product, and turning a problem into a solution, is in itself a tremendous capability. But when such opportunities reach a larger number of people in society, the balance of power becomes more equitable and its impact multiplies many times over. India's startup journey is an example of this transformation. Before 2014, India had only a few hundred startups. Today, the country has more than 250,000 startups. Nearly half of these startups come from small cities and towns. More than forty-five percent of startups have at least one woman Director or Partner. In other words, opportunities for innovation are reaching more people, new talent is emerging, and new solutions are being created. Friends, This focus on the purpose of power is equally important in governance. The government possesses authority, but its purpose should not be to make citizens dependent on the system. Rather, its objective should be to ensure that citizens become capable and empowered. India is moving along this very path. Following this path, India has lifted 250 million people out of poverty over the past decade, a number greater than the population of many countries in the world. Friends, You may also have heard about the JAM Trinity and Direct Benefit Transfer. By using technology, we have created a system through which government assistance reaches beneficiaries directly in their bank accounts. This has reduced corruption, increased convenience for citizens, and established a direct pathway between the government and the people. Friends, This same philosophy of Power to Empower is visible in India's global engagement. During the COVID pandemic, vaccine manufacturing capacity became extremely important. The question at that time was whether the benefits of this capability would remain limited to only a few countries, or whether vaccines would also reach those nations and people who lacked such facilities and capabilities. India chose the path of empowering humanity. We supplied vaccines to more than one hundred countries. Our manufacturing capability became a source of assistance for other nations as well. Let me give you another interesting example. A few months ago, India hosted the AI Impact Summit, and Mr. Bloomberg has just spoken very highly of that summit. There, we discussed not only the use of AI for humanity, but also how animals could benefit from AI. Nandan, who is seated here, can later share much more information about how this was done. In other words, the more capable and stronger India becomes, the more beneficial it will be for humanity and for the world. Friends, Power is not just the ability to move ahead. It is also the ability to empower others to move ahead. India believes that competition is important, but compassion is equally important. Innovation is essential, but its benefits must also be widely shared. National strength is necessary, but we must also contribute solutions to global challenges. Whether it is climate change, water governance, public health, or the responsible use of emerging technologies, the world needs capabilities that provide solutions and create new opportunities. Friends, Let me give you another example. Under the Paris Agreement, India made a major commitment to address climate change. In 2015, we set ourselves the target of achieving 40 percent of our installed electricity capacity from non-fossil fuel sources by 2030. India achieved this milestone in November 2021 itself, nine years ahead of schedule. India became the first G20 nation to do so. This was not merely about fulfilling a commitment; it was also a reflection of India's evolving energy journey. Friends, In 2014, India's renewable energy capacity stood at about 75 gigawatts. Today, it has reached approximately 300 gigawatts. In other words, we have added nearly four times more capacity. Friends, This energy transition underway in India is also creating new opportunities. Today, we have schemes such as PM Surya Ghar and PM-KUSUM. One scheme is connecting households to solar power, while the other is enabling farmers to generate solar energy and reduce their input costs through its use. Through these two schemes, India has achieved more than 30 gigawatts of solar capacity. It is estimated that they will reduce carbon emissions by more than 30 million tonnes annually. In other words, solar power is not only becoming a means of protecting nature, but also a means of empowering people. Friends, In this global race for power, India is moving forward with a different mindset. And this decade itself bears witness to that. Friends, This decade of the twenty-first century has brought unprecedented global challenges. If we look back at the last six years, we witnessed a global pandemic. Then wars broke out in different regions. As if that were not enough, disruptions emerged in trade, critical minerals, and energy supplies. The world begins to find a way out of one crisis when another crisis appears. Things that once connected the world are now being turned into weapons. Supply chains, energy, minerals, payment systems, and technology are increasingly becoming instruments of pressure and dominance. The routes created by globalization over past decades have, in many cases, become chokepoints. Amid all this, Artificial Intelligence is transforming the way every sector functions. At the same time, many countries are facing rapidly growing challenges related to ageing populations. In such circumstances, the world is confronted with a fundamental question: trust. Trust that supplies will continue during times of need. Trust that promises made will also be fulfilled. Trust that a partner country will stand by you during moments of crisis. And it is here that India stands before you with a different outlook and a different way of thinking. Friends, India's progress in the twenty-first century is a story of advancing together with everyone and carrying everyone along. When the world needs affordable medicines, one out of every five generic medicines comes from India. When solutions for digital identity and payments are required, the world looks to models developed in India. When the world needs talent, it places its trust in India's skilled youth. Today, India is home to more than 2,000 Global Capability Centres that are providing services to companies across the world. Friends, It is clear that whenever the world needs a steady pillar, India extends its hand in support, recognizing its responsibility. We do not grow at anyone's cost. We grow with the world, and for the world. And friends, India does not seek credit for what it does. The world proudly says that India gave Zero to the world. This Zero benefited all of humanity. Yet India never asked for royalties in return. Even today, India works with the same spirit. The ideas we offer for addressing contemporary challenges also reflect this outlook. Throughout history, many global organizations have been created, each serving its own interests. But consider the kind of global platforms India has created. Consider the institutions we have established and compare them with what has emerged elsewhere in the world. What have we contributed? The International Solar Alliance. What have we contributed? The Coalition for Disaster Resilient Infrastructure. What have we contributed? The Big Cat Alliance, the Global Biofuel Alliance, the India-Middle East-Europe Economic Corridor (IMEC), and Mission LiFE, Lifestyle for Environment. These are initiatives that benefit everyone and contribute to the well-being of all life. Friends, The ideas that India has brought forward are before you. The vision of One Sun, One World, One Grid. The belief in One Earth, One Family, One Future. The vision of One Earth, One Health. In other words, India places emphasis on connecting and coming together. And even in this, power is used to empower others. Friends, In a world filled with uncertainty, India offers the assurance of reliability and stability. In the April-June quarter of this year, India's growth rate stood at 7.8 percent. And this growth rests on a strong foundation. Twelve to thirteen years ago, India's current account deficit was more than four percent of GDP. Last year, it declined to approximately half a percent, standing at 0.61 percent. Today, our foreign exchange reserves exceed 700 billion dollars. Our banking system is in its strongest position in many decades. Friends, It is because of this strength that India's sovereign ratings are being upgraded. Today, India's confidence is at an unprecedented high. We are pursuing reforms with a clear policy direction. Through GST, we transformed a complex web of taxes into a national market. We enacted the Insolvency Law. Twenty-nine labour laws have now been consolidated into four simple labour codes. Hundreds of minor business-related offences have been decriminalized. And we have not stopped there. India's reform express continues to move ahead at high speed. For us, reforms are not a compulsion. Reforms are our conviction. Friends, Thousands of years ago, our Vedas gave us a mantra: "Sangachchhadhwam Samvadadhwam Sam Vo Manamsi Janatam. Let us repeat that mantra today: Let us walk together. Let us speak together. Let us strive to understand one another. Because when we walk together, our strength grows. Through dialogue, our understanding deepens. And by understanding one another, we can jointly solve great challenges. This is the essence of Power to Empower. It means not limiting our power solely to our own progress, but using it to create new opportunities for others as well. I am confident that this very platform of the Bloomberg Economic Forum will become a major platform for advancing this spirit. Once again, my very best wishes to all the participants for this event. Thank you very much. Thank you.
Excellencies Leaders of the BRICS business community, Innovators and friends, Namaskar! A warm welcome to all of you to the BRICS Business Forum. This year, we are celebrating twenty years of BRICS. When the group was launched in 2006, it comprised only four countries. Today, including BRICS and its partners, we have a family of twenty-one countries. Today, the BRICS countries represent 50% of the world's population, 40% of global GDP, and more than 25% of global trade. Not only that, over these years, while global GDP has grown by around 2.5 times, the combined GDP of BRICS countries has grown by around 4.5 times. In other words, the economic strength of BRICS has grown at nearly twice the pace of the world economy. BRICS countries are also emerging as important centres for global innovation and solutions. From grassroots initiatives to frontier technologies, new possibilities for innovation are taking shape across our countries. The growing scale, speed, and optimism of BRICS are clearly visible among us. The Forum being held in India is the largest-ever BRICS Business Forum. Friends, The theme of India's BRICS Presidency is "Building for Resilience, Innovation, Cooperation and Sustainability." Over the past twelve years, India has made this mantra the foundation of its economic and trade policy. When it comes to resilience, India has made its supply chains more diversified and reliable, from energy to technology. It is this very resilience that has enabled India, even amid global uncertainties, to offer the world confidence in growth and stability. We are rapidly expanding our roads, railways, ports, and airports. At the same time, we are building new capabilities in strategic sectors such as shipbuilding, semiconductors, quantum technology, critical minerals, and biotechnology. In these sectors, India is emerging as a hub of new opportunities for global businesses. Therefore, Make in India, Innovate with India, and Scale for the World. Friends, Today, India is witnessing a start-up revolution. With more than 200,000 start-ups and over 120 unicorns, India is emerging as a hub for global solutions. You will get a glimpse of this at the BRICS Bharat Innovates Expo. Our start-ups are transforming education and healthcare through AI, working on Green Hydrogen and Batteries, and breaking new frontiers in defence and space. Now, through BRICS as well, we must take these world-class innovations to global markets. Friends, Another distinctive feature of India's innovation story is Innovation at Population Scale. India's Unified Payments Interface (UPI) has given new momentum to financial inclusion and digital transactions at population scale. Today, 50% of the world's real-time payments are powered by this technology. To build new solutions on this digital layer, we have provided industry and start-ups with an open ecosystem. We are proud that many countries are adopting India's world-class ecosystem. Innovators from BRICS countries can also draw on this experience to develop new solutions for their own countries. Friends, Today, as trade barriers are increasing around the world, India is building economic bridges. Since 2014, we have entered into Free Trade Agreements with nearly 40 countries around the world. Our approach within BRICS is also the same, reduce barriers and expand opportunities for businesses. Therefore, under our Presidency, we have established new cooperation networks in areas such as agriculture, healthcare, skills, and smart grids. To connect start-ups and SMEs with markets and finance, we have launched initiatives such as the BRICS Incubator Network, BRICS MSME Portal, and BRICS Startup Innovation Fund. You can all benefit from these cooperation frameworks and further increase the exchange of investment and talent among our countries. Friends, Global trade can move forward only when sea lanes are secure, supply routes remain open, and seafarers are safe. That is why India is a staunch advocate of freedom of navigation and the safety of seafarers. The BRICS Women's Business Alliance is also an important part of our vision. We want women entrepreneurs to lead our growth story. We are very pleased that nearly 200 women business leaders participated in the meeting of this Alliance held in India. Friends, As BRICS enters its third decade, I would urge the BRICS Business Council to consider ... can you prepare a report on removing the top 10 trade barriers among BRICS countries? Can we support 100 BRICS startups every year to scale into other BRICS markets? Can we develop 1,000 new partnerships among our businesses? We should review our progress every year against these three benchmarks. Let us transform BRICS' ambition into action and our collective strength into global solutions. May BRICS move forward and help the world move forward as well. Thank you all very much.
My colleagues in the Union Cabinet, Dr. Jitendra Singh Ji, P. K. Mishra Ji, T. V. Somanathan Ji, Ajay Kumar Ji, all the esteemed members of the UPSC, Chairpersons of the various State Public Service Commissions who have come from different states, other distinguished guests, ladies and gentlemen. Before I begin today's discussion, I would like to mention an incident that occurred yesterday. The manner in which India's Captain Smita displayed patience, prudence, and courage, and as the Prime Minister of Israel said today, this Indian prevented another 9/11 from taking place. This act of valor by Smita fills every citizen of the country with pride. Today, I also spoke with his father Bhupendra Bhai, his mother Geeta Ben, and his wife Sonal. His treatment is ongoing, and today the entire world is proud of him. I appeal to the people of the country that we should all pray for Smita's long life and excellent health, and salute our brother Smita, who has enhanced India's glory. Friends, Today is not only a celebration of 100 years of the UPSC, but also a celebration of a legacy. It is a celebration of the aspirations that millions upon millions of young people cherish and pursue every year. It is a celebration of the trust that every Indian places in this institution. During this century-long journey, generations changed, governments changed, technology changed, and syllabi changed, yet the trust in the UPSC remains just as unwavering today, and that is one of your greatest assets. A commemorative postage stamp and coin marking this 100-year journey have just been released. A compilation of 100 important case laws has also been published. I pay my respects to all the distinguished individuals associated with this century-long journey. I extend my heartfelt congratulations and best wishes to all of you. A little while ago, I was visiting the gallery that has been set up here, and I would encourage all of you to spend 10 to 15 minutes exploring it. I noticed a photograph from the 75th anniversary celebrations in which Lt. General Surendra Nath can be seen. Today, at the age of 90, he is present among us. Friends, There are many individuals who transformed this journey into a legacy of trust, introduced changes in tune with the times, and took the institution to new heights. Every one of them deserves appreciation and recognition. While looking through the gallery, I noticed that it also contains information about how Subhas Chandra Bose went through this very process in 1920. What caught my attention was that although India was under British rule at the time, there used to be a Sanskrit paper in the examination, and Subhas Babu wrote his paper in Sanskrit. Friends, On this historic occasion, I would also like to pay tribute to Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel. After Independence, the challenge before him was not merely to keep the nation free. The challenge before him was also to govern and build a nation. The steel frame that was required for the reconstruction of the nation was provided by Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel. He made this institution one of the principal pillars of independent India and strengthened it. Friends, When the foundation of this institution was laid in 1926, the priorities of that era were different. At that time, both the focus of the system and its commitment were directed towards the British Raj. After India attained Independence, the dreams of a free India and the governance needs of a free India became the central focus. Over the past decades, the UPSC has discharged its responsibilities in an exemplary manner. Much has already been said from this stage about its achievements and contributions. Therefore, I neither wish to repeat those points nor go into detail about them. Moving beyond history, we now have India's future waiting before us. One quarter of the 21st century, twenty-five years, has already passed. The next twenty to twenty-five years will be extremely important for India, for every citizen of India, and for every institution of India. Therefore, I would like to focus my discussion today in that direction. Friends, For a Developed India, the kind of governance the country now requires depends significantly on the UPSC and the State Public Service Commissions. The young people whom you are recruiting today will play a crucial role in building a Developed India, and they themselves will be among the greatest beneficiaries of a Developed India. Keeping this in mind, we must always remember two touchstones. The first touchstone is: Nation First. The second touchstone is: Citizen First. These are the two standards against which every selection must be measured. When candidates are evaluated in this spirit, and when individuals imbued with this spirit are selected, the achievement of national goals becomes far more natural and far easier. Friends, India in 2047 will be very different from the India of today. India will be far more urbanized than it is today. The gap between villages and cities will narrow. The aspirations and expectations of our citizens, especially our youth, will be significantly greater. The signs of this transformation are already visible. Therefore, the question today is not merely one of service delivery. The question is also whether public services are able to keep pace with the speed of today's world of instant delivery. Are they able to move at that pace? Today, citizens do not want to wait weeks or months for a service. When AI can process thousands of documents within moments, and when governments have access to real-time data, citizens expect quick solutions. This changing mindset also demands new changes from our civil services. Therefore, we must align our selection parameters and recruitment processes with the needs of the future. We need to bring into public service those young people who are attracted not by public office, but by public service. Therefore, it is important that recruitment examinations should not merely assess how much a candidate knows. They should also assess why that young person wants to join the civil services. Because when the motivation is right, it becomes easier both to keep pace with changing public aspirations and to adapt to them. Friends, Many of you are familiar with the fact that whenever I meet trainee officers, I ask them to write down, right at the beginning and in their own handwriting, why they have joined the civil service. This is because responsibilities increase over time and new challenges emerge. At such moments, that small note reminds them of their original purpose. It also becomes a self-appraisal tool for them. UPSC and the State Commissions may also consider developing a similar mechanism. Friends, We have seen that period in the past when it was believed that the government was the ultimate guardian and provider. But today, our mantra is, "Nagrik Devo Bhava" (The Citizen is Divine). In other words, the citizen of this country is no less than a deity for us. Service to the citizen is our highest priority. Therefore, friends, Twenty-first-century India is moving forward with a new work culture. Its core principle is: Pro-People, Pro-Active, Good Governance! Pro-People means working not according to the convenience of the government, but by placing the interests of citizens above everything else. Pro-Active means preparing solutions by anticipating problems before they arise, instead of acting only after problems appear. Good Governance means there should be neither absence of government, nor pressure from government, nor unnecessary influence. At both the national and state levels, we must design our selection processes in accordance with this spirit. We must provide the nation with professionals who possess the sensitivity to understand the suffering of the poor; who willingly step forward with youthful energy; who find innovative solutions to difficult challenges; who understand the needs of the nation; who ensure compliance with the law; and who can also judge whether a law is making people's lives easier or whether it is becoming an obstacle. Friends, We are all witnessing the rapid pace at which the times are changing and technology is evolving. The period that lies ahead is going to present society with new kinds of challenges. We are all familiar with the global debate currently taking place around AI. In such a scenario, our civil servants too will be required to work under very different circumstances. There will be situations involving information warfare, the challenge of deepfakes, concerns regarding algorithmic bias, and complex questions such as climate justice. Therefore, in our selection processes, we must also give priority to assessing complex problem-solving abilities and long-term vision, and to making deliberate efforts in that direction. Friends, We all know that today our nation is moving rapidly on the expressway of reforms. At such a time, our examination-conducting agencies must move quickly on reforms that are the need of the hour. One such important issue is the time taken to conduct examinations. We must not view an examination merely as an event. It is connected not only to an event but to the future and lives of young people. A delay of even a few months in examinations can become a serious challenge for them. Coaching fees, rent, transportation expenses, all of these impose a heavy burden on families. Therefore, it is extremely important that our examination system adopts the principle of time certainty. UPSC and all selection commissions across the country must ensure that, without lowering standards, we find ways to shorten our examination cycle. In fact, while I was looking at the gallery, I noticed that nearly one hundred days have already been reduced. This achievement certainly deserves appreciation, and I congratulate all of you for it. But friends, This is not merely an administrative reform. It is also a reform that promotes equity. Friends, All of you are aware of the Chief Secretaries' Conference and the DGP Conference. Over the last ten years, these have evolved into important institutional mechanisms. Under these forums, all the Chief Secretaries of the country come together for three days to deliberate and exchange ideas. Similarly, during the DGP Conference, all the Directors General of Police gather for three days to discuss issues and share experiences. One objective of these conferences is to help one state learn about, understand, and, where appropriate, adopt the best practices of another state. I have been informed that every year UPSC organizes a National Conference in partnership with State Public Service Commissions. To make this conference broader, more productive, and richer in new ideas, I am sure you are continuously thinking about giving it a new shape and a new dimension. Such efforts help institutions share their best practices with one another. At the same time, this conference should also study international best practices. Focused discussions should also be held on how technology can be used to improve the efficiency of our processes and procedures. Such efforts at cross-learning will benefit UPSC as well as every state selection commission. We must also consider standardizing procedural norms and processes. This will provide significant assistance to young people. Friends, Another important subject is capacity building within UPSC and the state selection commissions. This effort should continue on a sustained basis. The iGOT platform developed under Mission Karmayogi can also be used for our internal systems, for strengthening the capabilities of our teams, and for enhancing the efficiency of our institutions through these various platforms. Friends, You must always remember that your work is linked directly to the future of India's youth. In other words, you are an important pillar in the mission of transforming youth power into national power. Over the years, you have played a major role in advancing this mission. When UPSC was established, opportunities were more readily available to young people from large cities, economically privileged families, and prestigious institutions. Today, however, youth from villages and small towns are also entering public service. The participation of our daughters is also steadily increasing. In 1952, women constituted only 3 percent of candidates in the civil services, just three percent. Today that figure has risen to more than 30 percent. In the entire history of UPSC, there have been four women Chairpersons. The time for applause comes now. Out of those four women Chairpersons in UPSC's entire history, three have been appointed after 2014. These changes are not merely statistics; they demonstrate how greatly the scope of opportunity has expanded in our country. Friends, In 1976, when UPSC completed fifty years, the number of candidates appearing for examinations was far lower than it is today. Compared to that period, the number of examination aspirants has increased by more than forty-five times. In other words, today we have before us an enormous pool of talent. This is a matter of immense pride for the nation. At the same time, our responsibility has grown proportionately. When we possess such a vast talent pool, one that is the envy of the world, you are not merely selecting a candidate. You are choosing an individual who will later become a representative of the government. At the grassroots level, people will regard that individual as the government itself. Therefore, all of you bear a tremendous responsibility to select the right person. Friends, During its first century, UPSC has continuously strengthened its credibility and earned the trust of the nation. Now, as this institution enters its second century, it must enrich and deepen that credibility in accordance with new possibilities and new challenges. While preserving the values that have been our strength until now, we must also upgrade ourselves to meet the demands of the future. UPSC's independence, impartiality, commitment to merit, confidentiality, and fair procedures constitute its defining identity. These values must always be preserved. With this appeal, I once again extend my heartfelt congratulations and best wishes to all of you. Thank you very much.
My colleagues in the Union Cabinet, Jyotiraditya Scindia Ji, Minister of State Chandrasekhar Pemmasani Ji, ministers from various state governments, all members of the GSMA Board, representatives of telecom operators from across the world, industry leaders, experts, my young friends who have come from different colleges and startups across the country, ladies and gentlemen. Today is October 8, a special day for India. We celebrate this day as Air Force Day. I pay my respects to all our brave Air Warriors and extend my greetings to the nation on Air Force Day. Friends, As Scindia Ji just mentioned, only yesterday I completed 25 years of serving the nation as the Head of Government. For me, this has been a natural and continuous journey of service. I express my gratitude to every citizen of the country and every friend of India across the world for their good wishes. Friends, It is also a coincidence that on this occasion, the India Mobile Congress is completing ten years. This year, India was chosen to host the GSMA Board meeting. I welcome and congratulate all of you representatives of telecom operators, industry leaders, experts, academia, and startups who have come here from across the world. Friends, This very morning, we received the news that the renowned actor Nana Patekar Ji is no longer among us. He was an outstanding artist who won the hearts of audiences through a wide range of roles. Nana Patekar Ji dedicated his life to the honour, pride, and glory of the Tricolour. He never allowed himself to be confined; like a bird, he soared across the skies of cinema. Through his films, he launched powerful attacks on disorder and injustice in society. He was a true revolutionary who worked against social evils. His words were powerful, and his silence was equally impactful. He consistently supported firm action against those who worked against the nation. I pay my tribute to Nana Patekar Ji and express my condolences to his family members and admirers. Friends, I have had the opportunity to join you at several editions of the India Mobile Congress. This platform has witnessed how the telecom sector and digital connectivity have evolved. In 2022, from this very stage, we launched 5G in the country. In 2023, I spoke about how India was moving from 5G Roll-out to 5G Reach-out. Today, we can all see that 5G connectivity has reached every district of the country. More than 550,000 base stations are now operating across India, providing 5G services. Mobile internet speeds have increased tenfold compared to 2022. Friends, At the 2024 edition of India Mobile Congress, we witnessed the presence of the International Telecommunication Union (ITU). At that time, we discussed the need for global standards for the digital world. Last year, we deliberated on Invest, Innovate, and Make in India. The position that India has achieved today in the telecom sector owes much to India Mobile Congress. Over the years, this platform has emerged as a stage for India's progress and India's aspirations. Therefore, I extend special congratulations to all of you. Friends, This year, you have chosen the theme of your deliberations as "Scale Without Boundaries." I would like to share India's experience of how we have turned the dream of scaling beyond boundaries into a reality. Friends, When we talk about telecom, discussions often revolve around networks, spectrum debates, towers, reach, and speed. But for the people of India, telecom has meant much more. Over the past decade, the mobile phone has emerged not only as a tool of modern communication but also as an important instrument of good governance. This has remained at the core of our Digital India vision. Friends, Today, we hear that data in India is affordable, mobile phones are affordable, and the user base has expanded dramatically. All of this is true. But how did it happen? And why was all this done? When we understand this, India's focus and priorities become crystal clear. Friends, Until a decade ago, the digital divide was a major challenge in India. Modern technology was increasingly becoming a privilege. In other words, only those with resources could benefit from modern technology. India was also moving in that direction. Through the Digital India campaign, we resolved to change this situation and placed the mobile phone at its centre. This was a major effort towards the democratization of technology. For this purpose, all necessary reforms were carried out in the telecom sector and infrastructure was expanded. We also focused on ensuring that mobile phones and other telecom hardware would be manufactured in India itself. Friends, As a result of these efforts, mobile phones and data became far more accessible and affordable for ordinary Indians. Today, this is enhancing the ease of living of our citizens. During the COVID period, we witnessed how almost everything came to a standstill. Yet offices continued functioning, banks remained operational, and education continued in India. This was made possible by India's digital ecosystem. Mobile and internet connectivity ensured uninterrupted access to education, healthcare, payments, and government services. Today, even children from remote villages and underprivileged backgrounds can access quality knowledge. Digital learning is increasingly becoming accessible to everyone. Friends, Let me share a statistic that further demonstrates the impact of Digital India. We have a platform called DIKSHA for online education. During the COVID period, by July 2022, more than 5 billion learning sessions and nearly 600 billion learning minutes had been recorded on this platform. Our effort now is to connect nearly 250 million students to all our digital and online education platforms. In other words, friends, The revolution in the telecom sector has directly benefited Indian citizens. As you know, India is diverse in every sense, in language as well as geography. Such diversity can also create many inconveniences in people's lives. Mobile connectivity has transformed this diversity into convenience. Earlier, most banks were located in cities, hospitals were concentrated in urban areas, and government offices were typically available only at block or district headquarters. Any government service required significant investment of both time and money. Digital governance has changed these conditions. Today, hundreds of government services are available through mobile phones. Government assistance is now transferred directly to beneficiaries' accounts. Through Direct Benefit Transfer, nearly ₹50 lakh crore has reached beneficiaries directly in their bank accounts. As a result, nearly ₹5 lakh crore has been prevented from falling into the wrong hands. This is not a small figure. Nearly ₹5 lakh crore has been saved from misuse. Now tell me, those whose easy earnings have stopped, will they love Modi? Did I do the right thing or not? Should this have been done or not? Should such wrong practices be stopped or not? They may shout and scream as much as they want, but I have made up my mind and I will continue in this direction. If the old system had remained in place, around ₹5 lakh crore of government money would have been lost through leakages and would have filled the pockets of such people. We stopped this. Now that money reaches the rightful beneficiary, and the savings are being utilized for productive purposes. Friends, Under the PM-Kisan Samman Nidhi scheme, we have already deposited more than ₹4 lakh crore directly into the bank accounts of farmers. This amount is transferred every four months, and the farmer receives a notification directly on his mobile phone. This money has reached crores of small farmers who, in the past, often remained beyond the reach of government schemes. In other words, friends, The mobile phone is no longer merely a medium for communication. It has ensured less travel, less waiting, less cost, and greater convenience. This is the greatest citizen dividend that technology can deliver. Friends, Over the past decade, India's telecom infrastructure has expanded continuously. Towers have been installed in remote areas, optical fibre networks have expanded on an unprecedented scale, and data has become more affordable. This has also given rise to entirely new sectors of the economy. It has created new consumers in technology, entertainment, and education, while simultaneously nurturing a new generation of creators. Take gaming as an example. It is estimated that today more than 500 million people in India are connected to the gaming ecosystem in one way or another. A large proportion of them are young Indians. Youth from Tier-2 and Tier-3 cities are also actively participating. They are not only playing games but competing in e-sports, creating gaming content, and building new gaming businesses. In other words, the mobile phone in India has become both a gaming console and a creation studio. Friends, The telecom revolution in India has brought a similar transformation in the world of content. Better and faster connectivity, coupled with affordable data, has taken video-sharing platforms, social media, and regional OTT services to villages and small towns. As a result, many language-related barriers and limitations have been removed. Today, the content creation industry alone is worth thousands of crores of rupees in India and provides income opportunities for millions of people. In other words, the mobile phone has democratized content creation in India. Friends, At a time when telecom and digital technology are playing such a critical role, it is equally important that we strengthen the security framework associated with them. The security of phones and the security of networks are priorities for all of us. An ordinary citizen does not think in terms of protocols or architecture; for them, trust is what matters most. When they receive a call, they should know who is calling. When they receive a message, they should know whether it is genuine or fake. When they make a payment, they should be confident that their money is secure. When they provide their data through a form, they should know that their information is protected. Friends, Technology achieves scale only when it earns trust. And we all know that cyber fraud and cybercrime do not respect borders. They have already crossed boundaries. A person sitting in one country may commit a crime in a third country with assistance from a second country. We are also witnessing how serious a challenge deepfakes have become. Therefore, it is necessary that we work on a global framework to address these issues. Governments, technology companies, telecom operators, and financial institutions must all become part of such a framework. I would also urge GSMA and telecom operators around the world to come together and create a global mechanism that can stop such fraud even before it reaches people. India has learned a great deal through its own experiences, and India is always ready to share those experiences with the world. Friends, Only four years remain in this decade. The discussions that take place at this edition of India Mobile Congress will strengthen the roadmap for the next decade. And this roadmap will guide not only India but the global mobile market as well. Over the past years, India has progressed from being a consumer to becoming a creator. Now India is moving towards a future where products and designs will originate in India, and their technology and intellectual property will also belong to India. To achieve this, every necessary incentive is being provided and every necessary reform is being undertaken. In other words, the reform express is moving rapidly forward in the telecom sector as well. We are modernizing laws that are decades old. Through the Telecommunications Act, we have created a modern regulatory framework. We are also rationalizing structural burdens such as AGR and bank guarantees, transforming burdens into opportunities. The GatiShakti Sanchar Portal has accelerated Right of Way approvals through digital processes. Friends, India's semiconductor and semiconductor manufacturing ecosystem is also gathering momentum. Commercial production has already started at five locations. In the next phase of the India Semiconductor Mission, our focus will be on the entire supply chain. In other words, India is taking major strides in every area associated with the telecom sector. Friends, Over the years, India Mobile Congress has achieved a great deal. However, the journey of development is one of continuously crossing new milestones. We have before us the grand resolve of a Developed India, and for that we must continuously broaden our efforts. The technologies showcased here certainly have their own importance. But IMC should be remembered for the partnerships that were forged here, for the research projects that were launched here, for the products that reached the world from here, and for the innovations that emerged from India and served humanity. We must transform IMC into a platform that serves as a junction for technology, research, industry, investment, and ideas. I am confident that all of you will continue to work towards this goal with complete dedication. With this expectation, I once again extend my heartfelt congratulations and best wishes to all of you. Thank you very much.
Union Minister in my Cabinet, Pralhad Joshi ji, Shripad Naik ji, all industry leaders, investors, colleagues from the startup world, senior officials, distinguished guests, ladies and gentlemen who have come from India and abroad. I welcome and greet all of you to the Bharat Renewable Energy Summit. This year's summit is special because it is being held under special circumstances. We have seen how this year has largely been dominated by discussions around energy shocks. The importance of renewable energy in our lives and in our development journey has become even clearer this year. It is also a happy coincidence that the 9th Assembly of the International Solar Alliance is being held alongside this summit. Delegates attending the Fourth Conference on Green Hydrogen are also present here. That means the scale of this year's event is very large. I extend my best wishes to all of you. Friends, Over the past few years, the global economy has witnessed unprecedented crises. From the COVID pandemic to the West Asia crisis, the world has faced several major challenges, and the crisis in West Asia is still continuing. But 140 crore Indians feel proud that we have handled these challenges better than many others, and despite these crises, India's growth has remained high. Had India not invested in clean energy and not established new systems in this sector over the past decade, our crisis could have been far more severe than that faced by the rest of the world. Friends, This global crisis has delivered a clear message to every country: energy security is directly linked to economic security. And we all know that the future of energy security lies in clean energy. If we continue to remain excessively dependent on fossil fuels, the global economy will continue to face repeated disruptions. Fossil fuel reserves are limited and concentrated in only a few regions. Renewable energy, on the other hand, has unlimited potential, and every region and every country can benefit from it. Friends, India's journey in this sector over the past twelve years has been one of promises, passion, and performance. The proof of this is our commitment made at COP-26. At the 2021 Summit, India resolved to achieve 500 gigawatts of non-fossil fuel capacity by 2030. We said: We will do it! In our Paris pledge, India had also set the goal of meeting half of its installed power capacity through non-fossil sources. India achieved that target itself in 2025. In other words, we achieved it five years ahead of schedule. Today, more than 50 percent of our installed power capacity is based on non-fossil sources. India is also progressing firmly towards the target of 500 gigawatts. We have already achieved nearly 300 gigawatts. And today, India is among the top three countries in the world in solar and renewable energy. Over the next ten to eleven years, our effort is to add another 500 gigawatts of new non-fossil fuel capacity. This target is somewhat challenging, but I have full confidence in the capabilities of my country. And therefore, I say: We will do it! Friends, We want India's innovations and India's experiences to benefit all humanity. This is also our approach towards green energy. India's successes in this field can be useful to many countries around the world. I will explain this through examples. Friends, In February 2024, we launched the PM Surya Ghar Muft Bijli Yojana. Under this scheme, the target is to install rooftop solar panels on one crore homes. In just the past twenty-four to twenty-five months, more than sixty-five lakh homes have joined this campaign. And as our Minister Shri Pralhad Joshi ji just mentioned, every six days, one lakh new homes become part of this solar movement. The impact of this can also be seen in electricity bills. One study shows that electricity bills for these sixty-five lakh households have fallen by nearly 76 percent. There are lakhs of families whose electricity bills have become zero. There are also many families that are generating surplus electricity and earning income by selling it back to the grid. This scheme can significantly strengthen energy security in many countries, especially in the Global South. Friends, India is making solar power a part of the lives of its citizens. Over the past twelve years, we have also tried to connect industries and farms with solar power. More than 44,000 government buildings in India are now connected to solar energy. This is saving nearly ₹700 crore annually. India's farmers are also benefiting greatly from the KUSUM Scheme. Through it, more than 30 lakh farmers have received access to solar-powered irrigation. It is estimated that every farmer benefiting from a solar pump is saving nearly one lakh rupees every year. Let me also give you the example of the PM Surya Sarovar Yojana. Its concept is to generate electricity by installing solar panels on ponds, lakes, and reservoirs. This allows better utilisation of water bodies. Such projects also reduce the costs associated with acquiring land. Friends, Our clean energy ecosystem is not limited only to hydro, solar, wind, and nuclear energy. India is moving in mission mode to take a major leap in the field of green hydrogen. Under the National Green Hydrogen Mission, we have set a target of producing five million tonnes of green hydrogen by the end of this decade. We are also connecting our transport sector with green hydrogen. Just a few months ago, India launched its first Green Hydrogen Train. Friends, All of you experts know that merely generating electricity is not enough. It is equally important to distribute it efficiently and deliver it where it is needed. In India, solar and wind energy are generated more abundantly in certain regions. We must ensure that areas where power generation is relatively low also benefit from it, so that homes, farms, and industries there can also run on clean power. That is why Green Energy Corridors are being developed. Under these corridors, a dedicated network of transmission lines and substations is being established. This is making it easier to transfer solar and wind power from places of production to places of demand. And in this effort, the contribution of One Nation, One Power Grid has been immense. Over the years, we have integrated five regional grids into a single national network. Today, the entire country is connected through a power grid operating at a common frequency. To further strengthen this Green Energy Corridor system, we have now moved to the next phase. Recently, India set an investment target of nearly ₹2 lakh crore for this purpose. Friends, A major reason behind our success in green energy is also our growing manufacturing capacity. Until a few years ago, we were completely dependent on imports even for the smallest components related to solar energy. A decade ago, our solar module manufacturing capacity was only around 2 gigawatts. Today, it has crossed 200 gigawatts. Solar cell manufacturing capacity has also reached nearly 40 gigawatts. We are now expanding the manufacturing of other components as well. In other words, India's ambitions in green energy are also proof of the success of our Atmanirbhar Bharat mission and the success of the Make in India campaign. Friends, India is striving to achieve a great deal within a very short span of time. We cannot stop here. We must connect every government building, every school and college, every hospital, and the entire street-lighting system to solar energy, and we must do so at a rapid pace. I would suggest that healthy competition should be encouraged among states and local bodies, so that those who perform better can be rewarded and others can learn from their success stories. Friends, I have a suggestion related to solar power in agriculture as well. We have distributed millions of solar pumps, but we now need to move beyond solar pumps. My vision is that the Annadata should also become an Urjadata - the food provider should become an energy provider. Farmers should install solar panels on unused land. Even the boundaries of fields should not remain unused; solar panels should be installed there as well. We must ensure that this reaches as many villages as possible. Friends, This new phase of the solar revolution is not only about adoption; it also depends on innovation. Therefore, I make a special appeal to our startups, IITs, innovators, and entrepreneurs: come forward and create models where as many household needs as possible can be met through solar power. We need solutions that make solar power available twenty-four hours a day and in every season. We must move towards a situation where a family can cook food using electricity generated by the solar plant installed on its own rooftop. We need to launch a clean cooking movement. Dependence on imported gas should be reduced as much as possible. We must also create a situation where a larger number of middle-class families have the option of electric vehicles and can charge their scooters, bikes, and cars at home using electricity generated from their own solar systems. This can save middle-class families thousands of rupees every year. Friends, We also need products that promote solar power in the dairy sector. Solar-powered equipment should be developed for every activity, from preparing animal feed to harvesting crops and post-harvest operations. Friends, None of this will be possible without next-generation innovation. For this, the country needs the necessary hardware. We need indigenous storage solutions. We need to build new battery storage capacity. This is a tremendous opportunity for all of you and for the youth of the country. I urge everyone in the world of research and all investors not to let this opportunity pass by. The solutions you develop in India will serve many countries around the world. Friends, India is one of the youngest countries in the world, and every effort in clean energy is creating large numbers of new employment opportunities. Even today, thousands and thousands of people are finding jobs in this sector, and there is tremendous potential for green jobs. These opportunities are being created across different fields, from manufacturing panels and other hardware to installation, servicing, and transportation. In the future, as solar power capacity increases further, just imagine how many more jobs will be created. From kitchen stoves to transportation systems, when everything becomes solar-powered and green-energy-driven, an entirely new industry will emerge. Friends, For all of you, for investors across the world, India is opening up enormous opportunities. It is difficult to find elsewhere the kind of demand, scale, and scope that India offers. It is equally difficult to find the kind of political and policy stability that India provides. And we are seeing the results of this. Over the past decade, more than ₹15 lakh crore, that is, 164 billion dollars, has been invested in the renewable energy sector. From the RE-INVEST Summit of 2024 until today, within just two years, investments worth nearly ₹6 lakh crore, that is, more than 63 billion dollars, have come into this sector. It is estimated that by the end of this decade, investment opportunities worth more than ₹20 lakh crore, approximately 220 billion dollars, will be created in this sector. Therefore, a new horizon of opportunities is opening up for all of you in India's clean energy sector. Friends, India has given the world the message of One Earth, One Family, One Future. The same philosophy is reflected in our clean energy journey. This is the thinking behind the International Solar Alliance. Our effort is to ensure that the funding, technology, and expertise required for renewable energy reach every country that needs them. That is why a proposal has been put forward for a 200-million-dollar Africa Solar Facility for Africa. Friends, The coming decade is extremely important for our climate goals. And I am confident that everyone present at this summit will play a major role in securing that future. We must connect every country to this mission. Every region must become a participant in this transformation. Only then will we be able to build a clean future, a green future, and a secure future. With these words, I once again extend my heartfelt best wishes to all of you. Thank you.
Most revered Pujya Swami Tejomayananda ji, Pujya Swami Swaroopananda ji, Pujya Swami Mitrananda ji, Pujya Swami Viviktananda ji, all revered saints, Acharyas, all dedicated sevaks of Chinmaya Mission, devotees and seekers who have arrived from across the country and abroad, ladies and gentlemen. Courtesy of all of you, today I have had the opportunity to participate in the Amrit Mahotsav of Chinmaya Mission. This is a unique spiritual experience. This is the special affection and blessing of our saints towards me. Because the Chinmaya Mission is not merely an institution; it is a Chinmaya ideal, which was established by a Chinmaya Rishi. Its journey over several decades has also been replete with pure, conscious resolutions. And whatever is Chinmaya is naturally immortal and imbued with nectar. Therefore, today at Bharat Mandapam, the name of this event - 'Chinmaya Amrit Mahotsav' - is equally meaningful. I extend my heartiest congratulations on the Chinmaya Amrit Mahotsav to the saints and Acharyas associated with the Chinmaya Mission across the globe, and to lakhs of seekers and followers. Friends, The feeling that arose in my heart upon arriving here today was made even more vibrant through modern technology. The darshan of Pujya Gurudev Swami Chinmayananda ji in this form, the privilege of conversing with his holobot, made it feel as though I had travelled 33 years back in time. My meeting with Gurudev in Vadodara in 1993, the conversation with him, his affection, his blessings - today everything reappeared before my eyes once again. For a moment, it felt as though I was once again in the direct physical presence of Gurudev. For me, this is a deeply emotional experience. I thank all the members of Chinmaya Mission from the bottom of my heart for this wonderful experience, and express my gratitude to them. Friends, Around February-March in 1993, I met Pujya Gurudev; I had gone to invite him to the centenary celebrations of the Parliament of the World's Religions in Chicago, USA. At that very time, 100 years of Swami Vivekananda ji's historic address were being marked. Pujya Gurudev accepted the invitation; Pujya Gurudev went to America, and across various programmes there, he consistently made people aware of the centenary celebrations of the World's Parliament of Religions and inspired them to take part. But destiny had ordained otherwise. Just a few weeks prior to the Parliament of Religions, on 3 August, Gurudev shed his mortal body in San Diego, America, leaving all of us behind. When the Parliament of Religions took place in Chicago on 11 September, fortunately, I was there. All of us felt the absence of Pujya Gurudev. Many words spoken by Gurudev kept echoing in my mind. The profound discussion that took place during my last meeting with him remains etched in my memory even today. Its core sentiment was: the building of youth power! And, through youth power, the creation of an empowered and capable India. Friends, A few years after meeting him, the sovereign people blessed me with the opportunity to serve as the Chief Minister of Gujarat. Since then until today, for the past 25 years, as the head of the government, I have been continuously serving the nation to the best of my ability; Guruji's blessings are with me. In this journey, the youth of the country stand at the centre of my efforts and policies. I am delighted that at the Chinmaya Amrit Mahotsav as well, the youth have shouldered major responsibilities. Many of our young companions undertook a journey of 40,000 kilometres over nearly 300 days. During this period, more than one lakh youths were contacted. I congratulate the entire team for this remarkable endeavour as well. Friends, The declaration of our Upanishads is: "Vayam Amritasya Putrah". That is, we are the children of immortality. This conception is not merely a spiritual philosophy; it is also our cultural conviction. This does not mean that our physical bodies are immortal; it means our consciousness is immortal. Our knowledge and our awareness are immortal. Because of this very awareness, this India has remained immortal for thousands of years. Because the very meaning of the word 'Bharat' is that which is engaged in the pursuit of knowledge and illumination. Therefore, friends, When the occasion of 75 years of the country's independence arrived, the thought came to my mind that we must not only turn this occasion into an inspirational platform for a developed India, but we must also revive that essence of India which knows how to live with immortal ideas, and which, through its experience of thousands of centuries, can care for humanity across eras. That is why I called for celebrating that occasion as the Amrit Mahotsav. And see, with the passage of time, one wondrous journey after another has continued to align with that resolution. Numerous spiritual, cultural, and social organisations took Amrit resolves at key milestones of their respective journeys during this period. And now, Chinmaya Mission is also joining the mission of India's Amrit Kaal in the form of its Amrit Mahotsav. And this indeed is the true strength of India's resolves; this is the genuine proof of our being "Amritasya Putrah". Friends, The call of our Vedas is: "Vayam Rashtre Jagriyama Purohitah" - meaning, may we, who seek the welfare of the nation, keep the nation awakened and elevated. Swami Chinmayananda ji lived his entire life guided by this very mantra. With as much devotion as he spoke on spiritual matters, with equal promptness he delivered articulate discourses on national issues. Because our nation is the birthplace of our culture. This nation is the very foundation of our existence. Swamiji always taught that we should link our spiritual advancement with personal growth, and make personal growth the vehicle for national progress. To fill the people of India with self-respect towards India was Swamiji's mission. With this objective, he initiated the Gita Jnana Yajna. He began the campaign of taking the wisdom of the Upanishads, Vedanta, and our scriptures to the common people. And today, we can see the results of that resolution of his in many ways. Due to the efforts of Chinmaya Mission, today there are lakhs of youth who are serving as cultural brand ambassadors of India. These youth are passing on Indian values to the new generation. This contribution of Pujya Gurudev is an important chapter in India's cultural and spiritual renaissance. Friends, Today, I also recall the occasion of Pujya Gurudev's birth centenary. In 2015, I had the privilege of releasing a special commemorative coin in his memory. Even then, I had remarked that Swamiji's life was a wonderful confluence of knowledge, action, and devotion. His Guru, Swami Sivananda ji, had blessed him with the words: "Go, roar like a lion." He lived his entire life in that very spirit. The confidence and inner strength in his speech left an enduring impression of his thoughts on everyone's heart. His radio programme named 'Art of Man Making' provided direction to countless individuals. Friends, The mission of great personalities remains alive even after they depart. That is why today we see that more than 100 educational institutions of Chinmaya Mission are shaping the future of lakhs of children and youth. In these institutions, they are receiving modern education while also assimilating Indian values. Friends, The vision that visionary individuals like Swami Chinmayananda ji held at the time of independence - today the nation is fulfilling its responsibilities in that very direction. To ensure that our education system aligns with the needs of a new India, we introduced the National Education Policy. In this new Education Policy, priority has been accorded to Indian knowledge traditions, education in the mother tongue, modern science, and new technology. Friends, Another mission of Swamiji's life was: pride in Indian heritage and its preservation! All of you are familiar with the Vivekananda Rock Memorial in Kanyakumari. The resolution to build a memorial on that massive rock amidst the sea was taken by Shri Eknath Ranade ji. At that time, Pujya Gurudev stood firmly by his side. The first financial contribution for this great endeavour was also made by Pujya Swamiji. Today, the Vivekananda Rock Memorial stands as a centre of inspiration for lakhs of people visiting from across the country and the world. Friends, Today, from north to south, east to west, India's cultural centres are undergoing a renaissance. Be it the renewed form of Kashi Vishwanath Dham, the grand temple of Lord Shri Ram in Ayodhya, the Mahakal Mahalok of Ujjain, or our shrines like Kedarnath and Badrinath. If you have visited Kedarnath ji recently, a divine, grand statue of Adi Shankar, Adi Shankaracharya ji, has been established there, where Adi Shankar shed his mortal coil. Today, they are returning to their majestic form. All of you know that at one time, so many statues were stolen from India, taken abroad, and sold there. There was no one to stop it, no one to inquire - leave aside those who worshipped them, there was no one even to ask. Over the past 12 years, we have brought back more than six hundred and fifty such stolen ancient statues. Our cultural centres are becoming part of UNESCO World Heritage Sites. Friends, Just as Swamiji took our dharma and philosophy to the people of the world, similarly, today International Yoga Day is connecting people across the globe with India and with our ancient, glorious tradition. This confidence of taking pride in our heritage, this assurance of liberation from the mindset of subjugation, is evidence that the nation is now advancing on the path envisioned for centuries by our rishis and patriots. Their strength of thought is our greatest asset along this path. Friends, Different institutions and organisations have their respective objectives, but the one purpose that unites everyone, the destination where all paths converge, that goal is: the development of the nation, the pride of the nation. In Swami Chinmayananda ji's time, according to the challenges that lay before the country, he shaped his resolutions. In the passing decades, times have changed, new challenges have arisen before the nation, and new distortions have entered society. Today, the responsibility of finding solutions rests upon all of us. Today, we have to unite society against divisive forces, and make our fellow citizens aware of self-reliance. We have to connect every individual with the resolution of a Developed India by 2047. We must become even more proactive towards these objectives. Friends, In 2047, when India completes 100 years of its independence, around that very period, Chinmaya Mission will also celebrate its centenary year. That is to say, Chinmaya Mission's centenary journey and the journey towards a Developed India will progress together. Therefore, our goals are one, our objectives are one, and we must take this journey forward together. You possess the great legacy of Gurudev and decades of experience. You have a vast family of dedicated Acharyas, seekers, and workers across the globe. I am confident that all of you together will play an important role in building a Developed India. With this conviction, I once again bow at the feet of Pujya Gurudev. I extend my heartiest congratulations on the Amrit Mahotsav to all the saints, Acharyas, seekers, and service-oriented companions of Chinmaya Mission. Hari Om. Hari Om. Hari Om. Thank you very much.
My Greetings to everyone! Governor of Karnataka, Sudha Yadav Ji, Chief Minister D. K. Shivakumar Ji, my Cabinet colleague, Nirmala Sitharaman Ji, Minister in the State Government, Basavaraj Rayareddy Ji, my colleague in Parliament, Yaduveer Krishnadatta Chamaraja Wadiyar Ji, all the scientists, researchers, teachers, other distinguished dignitaries, and my dear brothers and sisters of Mysuru. First of all, I respectfully bow to Tai Chamundeshwari. I am very happy to be here in Mysuru once again. Just a few months ago, I had come among you for the inauguration of the Viveka Smaraka. And today, we have gathered here to dedicate the COSMOS Planetarium to the nation. Both these occasions convey a profound message about India's values and our culture. Viveka Smaraka inspires us to look within ourselves and seek spiritual knowledge. At COSMOS, on the other hand, our eyes are lifted towards the sky. We are inspired to explore this vast universe and understand its mysteries. This is an example of how the traditions of spirituality and science in India have always progressed side by side. Friends, Our ancestors used to study the sky very closely. They understood the movements of stars and planets, prepared calendars and calculated the timings of solar and lunar eclipses. India was home to many great astronomers such as Aryabhata, Varahamihira, Bhaskaracharya and Nilakantha Somayaji. The advanced instruments and technologies that are available today did not exist at that time. Despite this, they made great discoveries and carried out even the most difficult calculations. Friends, Mysuru has a tradition of establishing institutions that encourage the pursuit of knowledge. The University of Mysore was established during the reign of Maharaja Nalwadi Krishnaraja Wadiyar. The Wadiyar family also played an important role in laying the foundation of the Indian Institute of Science in Bengaluru. These institutions have provided generations of students and researchers with opportunities to advance. And today, when India is achieving one milestone after another in the field of science, Mysuru, too, is adding a new chapter to India's scientific journey in the form of this state-of-the-art COSMOS Planetarium. Today, this state-of-the-art COSMOS Planetarium is being dedicated to the youth and the people of the country. It is India's first LED Dome Planetarium. I have just had the opportunity to watch a show here. It was a remarkable experience in itself. I congratulate the Indian Institute of Astrophysics, the University of Mysore, various government departments and everyone associated with its construction. I extend my congratulations to the nation on this occasion. Friends, For a long time, there has been a call in our country, which Nirmala Ji mentioned in her speech just now - Jai Jawan, Jai Kisan. Atal Ji added Jai Vigyan to it, and then I added Jai Anusandhan as well. The India of the 21st century is moving forward with this very vision. And our country's youth are performing exceptionally well in this regard. Last year, India hosted the International Olympiad on Astronomy and Astrophysics in Mumbai. Our young people participated in it with great enthusiasm. And this year, for the next edition of the same Olympiad, five students from India went to Vietnam. Four of them won gold medals and one won a silver medal. This is a matter of pride for the entire country. Friends, Our young students are also performing remarkably well in other fields of science. This year, Indian students achieved major successes in the International Olympiads as well. These included subjects such as Mathematics, Physics, Chemistry and Biology. All the students won either gold or silver medals. This has been a record achievement for India. This means that when our youth get opportunities, when they receive the necessary resources to learn, and when they get the support of technology, tools and institutions, they demonstrate their ability to achieve great things. This COSMOS Planetarium, too, is now going to help them greatly in giving shape to their dreams. Friends, Technology is changing the world very rapidly today. And no country can afford the risk of moving slowly in this area. Over the past decade, India has made unprecedented progress in astronomy, astrophysics and the space sector. We have installed new telescopes in the country. In several challenging areas of space exploration, India has today become one of the few countries in the world. Through Chandrayaan-3, ISRO made India the first country in the world to land near the Moon's South Pole. In the form of Aditya-L1, India has received its first solar observatory in space. Just last year, India became the fourth country in the world to demonstrate the capability of docking and undocking in space. Friends, During this period, an Indian also reached the International Space Station and conducted several new experiments. The experience gained from this mission is also proving useful for our own human spaceflight programme, Gaganyaan. Friends, Our government opened up the space sector. This created new opportunities for the private sector. India's startups also got an opportunity to demonstrate their capabilities. Today, our youth are launching their own rockets, placing satellites into orbit and undertaking many such activities in the space sector that had never been carried out in India before. This is the scientific approach of a New India, where new discoveries are being encouraged, new technologies are being developed and new opportunities are being created for our youth. Friends, India has always moved forward with the mantra of Humanity First. India's space missions, too, have been dedicated to the welfare of humanity. For example, ISRO has developed a satellite communication system for our fishermen brothers. With the help of this system, when boats equipped with receivers are at sea, officials can send timely alerts to them during storms. And they can also track their safe return to the shore. Friends, Many of you must be checking the live location of trains. This tells you where a train has reached and when it is expected to arrive at a station. This information is provided by the Real-Time Train Tracking System developed by Indian Railways and ISRO. Similarly, satellite data is also used in the Pradhan Mantri Fasal Bima Yojana. Under this, information collected from fields, along with satellite data, is used to estimate crop yields. Farmers' insurance claims are being determined on the basis of these estimates. This means that today, our satellites are reaching the heights of space, while at the same time, they are being used to improve people's lives on Earth. Friends, Science for Society- this is our approach. We are continuously promoting research and innovation in the country. More than one crore students have so far benefited from the country's over 10,000 Atal Tinkering Labs. These labs provide children with opportunities to work on their ideas, conduct new experiments and innovate. Friends, To advance research, it is essential that our students and researchers have access to the world's best research. With this objective, we launched One Nation One Subscription. It is now greatly helping the country's educational and research institutions gain access to leading scientific journals from around the world. We established the Anusandhan National Research Foundation. Through this, we are helping an increasing number of universities undertake new research. Friends, Last year, we also launched a Research, Development and Innovation Scheme worth Rs. 1 lakh crore, so that our country's researchers continue to receive adequate funding. This is helping take new technologies out of laboratories and bring them to practical use. Friends, Today, a strong ecosystem for science and research is taking shape in the country. Curiosity among young people is essential for making progress in the field of science. Planetariums and science centres play an important role in nurturing this curiosity. I urge everyone associated with COSMOS to work in this area so that schools and colleges here can organise visits for as many students as possible, and arrangements can be made to bring them here. The children who come here to learn about the universe may well be among those who return here one day and tell us about the new discoveries they have made about the universe.
