Jai Jagannath. Governor of Odisha, Shri Brajmohan Ram ji; popular Chief Minister of the state, Shri Mohan Charan Majhi ji; my colleagues in the Union Government, Smt. Nirmala Sitharaman ji, Shri Jual Oram ji; Governor of the Reserve Bank of India, Shri Sanjay Malhotra ji; Deputy Chief Ministers of Odisha, Smt. Pravati Parida ji, Shri Kanak Vardhan Singh Deo ji; distinguished guests associated with the banking and financial sector, ladies and gentlemen. Why is the atmosphere so serious? Does banking carry this much pressure, does technology carry this much pressure? Please sit comfortably. With the blessings of Mahaprabhu, today Bhubaneswar is giving a new institution to a New India. The inauguration of both the RBI's Data Centre and the Cybersecurity Training Institute in Bhubaneswar marks an important occasion. This is going to be of immense benefit to the people of Odisha and the entire country. I extend my heartiest congratulations to the RBI and everyone associated with this project. Friends, Today's programme bears testimony to how the approach to governance in India is transforming and how rapidly it is evolving. And our focus within this is: priority of development, expansion of development, and reaching the last person with development. Friends, After independence, large cities were developed in the country, grand institutions were established, large hospitals were built, and massive airports were constructed. Yet, a vast section of the country kept waiting for basic amenities. Delhi had large hospitals, but people in smaller cities and remote areas had to travel long distances for better medical treatment. Large cities enjoyed air travel facilities, but connecting smaller towns with air connectivity was not given adequate focus. Big cities had some amenities, whereas in numerous villages, meeting basic needs such as roads, electricity, and water remained a formidable challenge. Railway stations formed the most crucial link of the common citizen's travel, yet modern amenities were lacking at these stations. In other words, ensuring that the benefits of development reached every region and every citizen equally remained a major hurdle. We made an effort to transform this very mindset. We proceeded with a model of Pro-People, Pro-Active, Good Governance. For us, development denotes balanced progress across India - opportunities for villages as well as smaller towns. Our goal is to extend better connectivity to far-flung areas, provide facilities for underprivileged families to live with dignity, and continually instill confidence in every citizen that they will not be left behind in the journey of development. Today, the nation's expressways are linking smaller cities. Premier institutions like AIIMS have expanded across multiple states. Medical colleges are being opened in smaller towns. Nearly 1.75 lakh Ayushman Arogya Mandirs have been established in villages. Digital connectivity is expanding into remote regions. This is the true purpose of governance now: making citizens' lives easier and offering new avenues for their aspirations. Friends, Our banking sector has also stood as a prime example of how ordinary families, the poor, and the middle class were kept deprived for decades. In the 1960s and 70s, banks were nationalised in the name of the poor. Yet, until 2014, more than half of the country's population was excluded from the banking ecosystem. Financial products such as bank loans were a distant dream even for the middle class; the poor, small farmers, the landless, and our sisters and daughters could not even fathom accessing them. Today, digital banking in India has connected everyone to 24-hour financial services. But prior to 2014, there was an entirely different era of 'phone banking'. A select few in Delhi and those connected with influential circles were granted loans worth thousands of crores through phone calls. Poor and middle-class families required a guarantor merely to open a single bank account, whereas under the Congress's phone banking arrangement, thousands of crores of rupees were available anytime for the wealthy. It was the consequence of this very mismanagement that pushed India's banking system into the grip of NPAs, driving it to the brink of ruin. Friends, For any substantial transformation, having an expansive mindset is essential; when intentions are pure and motives are noble, tasks that seem impossible turn into reality. Where vision is narrow, possibilities also shrink. The frameworks built by the nation in recent years, along with the new ecosystem developed under Digital India, have extended banking to every single citizen. Today, India's banking sector stands among the strongest in the world. Earlier, through phone banking, only those with access to a privileged family in Delhi benefited. Today, through digital banking, every household across the nation has become a beneficiary. Friends, It was from this very land of Odisha that a former Prime Minister of the country once stated that when one rupee is sent from Delhi, only 15 paise reaches the people. That was a statement from a Prime Minister. That was one era, and this is today. Today, when one rupee leaves Delhi, the entire one rupee reaches the bank account of every beneficiary. As Nirmala ji mentioned just now, this has been made possible solely because of the approximately 59 to 60 crore Jan Dhan accounts opened in the country. Friends, Today, even someone who has nothing to offer the bank as a guarantee is able to secure an affordable and accessible loan to start their own work. From small entrepreneurs to traditional artisans, from street vendors to workers in the unorganised sector, they have received the benefits of formal banking for the first time. Through the Mudra Yojana, 13 crore people have become first-time entrepreneurs. PM SVANidhi has provided loans to lakhs of street vendors for the first time. The PM Vishwakarma scheme has connected lakhs of skilled craftspersons with banks. Banks have also extended assistance worth thousands of crores of rupees to self-help groups comprising 10 crore rural women. Friends, This is the same nation where debit and credit cards were once symbols reserved for the wealthy. Today, they are accessible to every citizen. Crores of RuPay credit and debit cards have been issued in the country, becoming symbols of both self-respect and self-reliance. All of this has been achieved through your collective cooperation. Be it the RBI, every bank in the nation, or each bank employee serving within them - the work you have accomplished makes you truly deserving of this success. Friends, Over the past decade, alongside financial empowerment, the social security net has witnessed an unprecedented expansion. Through the Atal Pension Yojana, 9 crore people have received social security coverage, with 38 lakh individuals covered in Odisha alone. About 10 to 12 years ago, insurance was considered a luxury; the poor and middle class could hardly contemplate it. For us, 'Nagrik Devo Bhava' (The Citizen is Divine) is the guiding mantra of good governance. To provide protection during accidents, we introduced the PM Suraksha Bima Yojana. At a nominal premium of just ₹20 per year, an insurance coverage of up to ₹2 lakh is being provided. Similarly, for life insurance, we introduced the PM Jeevan Jyoti Bima Yojana, available at an annual premium of less than ₹450. Crores of Indians are enrolled in both these schemes. To date, claims exceeding ₹25,000 crore have been disbursed to the public. If any other government had announced the distribution of ₹25,000 crore, it would have generated massive headlines, accompanied by celebrations and fanfares for months. However, under our administration, this support reaches the poor and the middle class during difficult times silently and without fanfare. This is what defines good governance. Friends, The past decade focused on extending banking access to those who were excluded. The coming decade belongs to making banking more efficient and more dependable. In this effort, this new Data Centre in Odisha will play a major role. If we are to keep the banking system secure, its digital infrastructure must also remain protected. For this reason, we require robust data centres and empowered computing systems. And friends, We all understand that modern warfare is not waged solely with weapons. Weakening a country can also be attempted by striking at its economy. As India gains financial strength, safeguarding our banking system against cyber criminals and cyber terrorists is equally imperative. The nature of cyber threats is evolving today. We cannot counter such emerging challenges using outdated systems, obsolete training modules, or legacy tools. This necessitates modern infrastructure alongside skilled human resources. It is precisely here that the role of this new Cybersecurity Training Institute becomes critically important. Friends, These new facilities of the RBI also reflect a fresh priority for India. Eastern India is emerging as a powerful growth engine for a developed India. Following the formation of the double-engine government in Odisha, progress has gained new momentum. Alongside its natural resources, Odisha's economy is now being fortified by technology. On one hand, mineral-based industries are expanding; on the other hand, concerted efforts are underway to foster a new ecosystem for semiconductors and electronics. You will witness that Odisha is set to script a new future, spanning from mines to microchips. Fresh investment prospects are emerging in Odisha across data centres and advanced computing, opening numerous avenues for its youth. I assure the young people of Odisha that the big dreams you have envisioned will certainly be fulfilled. We will do it; we are determined to accomplish this. With this spirit, I once again extend my heartfelt congratulations to all of you. Best wishes to everyone. Jai Jagannath!
RBI Governor Shri Sanjay Malhotra ji, Global Fintech Fest Chairman Kris Gopalakrishnan ji, all innovators, leaders and investors of the fintech world, ladies and gentlemen. I welcome all guests and delegates who have come from India and abroad to the Global Fintech Fest. I am happy that today another hat‐trick has been achieved. One hat‐trick was in 2024 when the government was formed for the third time, and another hat‐trick is my presence among you. This is my third consecutive year at the Global Fintech Fest. This hat‐trick shows how important you are for a developed India, how important your work is. Friends, Every time I come here, I see more youth, I feel more energy. I see the scope of possibilities expanding. It is clear that risk‐taking capacity is increasing, dreams are becoming bolder. And therefore, my confidence in the future of developed India is becoming stronger. Friends, Amidst this energy of fintech, Mumbai is also experiencing cultural energy at its peak these days. In just a few days, Ganeshotsav will begin in Maharashtra and across the country. In our tradition, Lord Ganesh is remembered before every auspicious work. Bowing to Ganapati Bappa, I extend my best wishes to all of you for Global Fintech Fest 2026. Friends, You all know well what the global situation is. The world is passing through a period of conflicts and uncertainty. Energy and commodity supply chains are under pressure, there are tensions in trade. These conditions have persisted for many months. The quarter from April to June was the most challenging. And even in such a situation, when India grows at 7.8 percent, the world gains new confidence. And this trust, I can clearly see on your faces here. Friends, Another example of this growing trust in India is our sovereign ratings. Recently, a Japanese credit rating agency upgraded India's sovereign ratings to the A category. An upgrade is a matter of happiness, but very few people know that this fortune has come after three decades - after thirty years. This shows how assured the world is about India's growth momentum, our macro‐economic stability, and the structural reforms carried out in recent years. And I repeat, India's reform express will move forward at even greater speed. Friends, India's fintech story has been extraordinary. And one of its biggest champions is our UPI. I spoke about it last year too. But this transformation is so big that whenever fintech is discussed, it will always be incomplete without UPI. Friends, Just a few days ago, on 25th August, UPI completed 10 years. Ten years ago, when I said that your bank would be at your fingertips, some people thought it impossible - and I am talking about well‐informed people, remember their faces. I am not talking about the millions of Indians whose bank accounts had just been newly opened then. Generations of theirs had never even seen the door of a bank. But today, ten years later, look - those same millions of people, who for generations were outside the banking system, have become the big drivers of fintech growth in India. Friends, Today, in the month of August alone, more than 2,400 crore transactions were done through UPI. That means, in just the time we have been sitting here, in 10 minutes alone, on average more than 5 million UPI transactions have taken place across the country. This is the impact because of which France's President Macron said - he said that UPI is not just a tech story, it is a civilizational story. And friends, UPI is no longer limited to India. A few years ago, if someone had said that payments could be made abroad directly from an Indian bank account, hardly anyone would have believed it. But today, it is a reality. India's UPI is now live in 11 countries. These are extraordinary figures, showing how long a journey we have traveled. But now the question is - should we be satisfied with this journey? Should we consider fintech's success in India as final? Friends, I am completing 25 years in the ecosystem of policy and governance. And those who have seen me in these years know - Modi is not one to stop at a milestone, celebrate, and be satisfied. When one goal is achieved, I immediately begin preparing for an even bigger goal. Friends, Today our UPI has reached many countries. But this is only the first step. Our next goal should be to connect with the payment systems of those countries. We have already done this with Singapore. Because of this, transactions between India and Singapore happen just like between two neighborhoods of the same city. This same connection we must build with other countries, other markets. We must move forward with this goal. Wherever Indians live in large numbers, wherever India trades on a large scale, whichever country is ready to walk with us - we must work to connect them all. Friends, This is also necessary because abroad, a large number of Indians work. And Indians are the largest in the world in sending money back home. But today, a part of that money is lost in transaction costs. UPI can save Indians even in this, and money can reach Indian families more quickly. Friends, Today, how do you make payments in India with cards? This system runs on standards that were designed elsewhere. We adopted those standards and rules that others had created. At that time, we did not have our own option. But now we have our own option. Now we can create our own rules and standards, and connect them with the world. I have full confidence in all of you that you can do this. Friends, Technology is the most effective tool of inclusion. And UPI has demonstrated this. When it comes to fintech, its unique feature must be to reach where traditional models could not. Where traditional banks could not reach, where bank branches could not reach, UPI has taken the banking system to that place, to that person. Friends, Today, a large part of the fintech market is only payments. But now we must move to the next level, and move quickly. In the fintech market, along with payments, the share of credit transactions must increase, the share of insurance must increase, the share of savings, investments, and pensions must increase. Friends, I believe fintech can bring out that strength of the country which is still outside the reach of traditional financial models, or not being addressed properly. For a developed India, bringing out this strength is very necessary. Friends, We can already see the impact of this in the success of PM SVANidhi. Our street vendors are the backbone of cities. But they were outside the country's banking system, outside the formal credit system. PM SVANidhi connected millions of street vendors to the formal system. And now these companions also have the SVANidhi credit card - that means they have a tool of formal credit access. And studies show that the average annual income of these beneficiaries has increased by more than 20 percent. Not only this, their families have seen improvements in housing, nutrition, healthcare access, and children's education. That is why the government has decided to extend this scheme until 2030. Friends, PM SVANidhi would not have achieved this success if UPI did not exist. UPI has played a very big role in the success of this scheme. Learning from this experience, we must use the power of fintech to further increase economic activities. Let me give you an example from the world of capital and credit. There is a sister who runs a small shop. She receives digital payments every day. Now her income and expenses show a clear pattern. Her business is running well. She has moved beyond the level where small capital was enough. Now she needs goods, equipment to scale up her business, and for this she needs a little more money. This is not very large credit. And her business is still so small that traditional credit models cannot serve her efficiently. There are many such businesses whose needs fintech can analyze, anticipate, and address. And the number of such businesses is very large. Friends, Technology can help us study the economic activities of such businesses and design curated models for them. I believe the next chapter of fintech will be written here. We have shown speed and scale in payments. Now we must help increase the share of non‐payment transactions. For example, a delivery partner, or a person doing some small work - he earns every month, but he needs access to services like savings and pension products. Fintech can address the needs of such people. Similarly, fintech can make access easier to insurance products and other financial services. All these can be made just as simple, easy, and accessible as making a payment today with a QR code. Friends, The first quarter of the 21st century has ended. Now we are in the year 2026. The second quarter has brought even newer possibilities for us. Agentic AI, tokenization, quantum - such technologies are opening new doors of possibilities in the financial sector. Now the challenge before us is how to turn these possibilities into real impact. And for this, I want our fintech companies to set some tasks for themselves. And I want to place some suggestions before you. First - we must make cyber security in India top‐notch. Second - our industry must have its own ethical data protection standards. Third - we must build a strong fintech regulator‐industry innovation ecosystem. And fourth - we must have a fintech consumer protection index, on the basis of which every company can have a transparent rating. If we test ourselves on these parameters, India's fintech sector can become the gateway of countless possibilities. We have the experience, and we have the way to implement it on the ground. Therefore, we must work quickly. Friends, The success story of Digital India is now ready for a new chapter. The success of our fintech story so far has been scale, scope, secure systems, democratization of technology, and public trust. Keeping these core values in mind, the fintech sector must focus on next‐generation innovation. With this appeal, once again I extend my best wishes to all of you. Thank you very much.
Union Minister in my Cabinet Dr. Jitendra Singh ji, Principal Scientific Adviser to the Government of India Ajay Sood ji, all Nobel Prize-winning scientists, all other distinguished guests, ladies and gentlemen. Today, such a large number of delegates from the world of Science and Technology are present here. Our young innovators are here as well. Before coming here, I interacted with some startup founders. The scale at which India's youth are thinking and the confidence with which they are moving forward is truly encouraging. Friends, I had attended this conclave last time as well. This time, we are discussing the subject of Emerging Science and Technology. Decades ago, there was a time when Science and Technology, for India, meant catching up. Whenever something new was innovated in the world, that technology would reach us after a few years. We would adopt it or make minor improvements and move forward. But today, 21st-century India is moving ahead with a new mindset and a new confidence. Now our goal is to create new technologies for India, for the world, for today, and for the future, to determine their direction, and to develop the capability to provide solutions to global challenges. During the COVID period, we saw an example of this. When many countries around the world were struggling with vaccine availability, India developed its own vaccine, carried out vaccination on a large scale, and also supplied vaccines to other nations. This is the confidence of 21st-century India. We reach Mars in our very first attempt. We reach the South Pole of the Moon, a place where no one had reached before. And that is why I firmly believe: India must embrace the technologies of the future, innovate boldly, and lead the world in creating them. Friends, From the discovery of zero to the number system and astronomy, India's tradition has always been one of knowledge and discovery. Today, we are taking this scientific heritage forward with modern technologies and renewed confidence. Some examples from the past year help us understand this transformation even better. On 30th October, the nation will celebrate the birth anniversary of Dr. Homi Jehangir Bhabha. Therefore, let me begin with nuclear energy. Friends, All of you know that for building a Developed India, we will require nuclear energy on a large scale. Dependence on imports for nuclear fuel is a challenge to our energy security. Therefore, India is rapidly advancing its Fast Breeder Reactor Technology. Just a few months ago, the country's 500-Megawatt Prototype Fast Breeder Reactor achieved First Criticality. This is an extremely significant and important step towards strengthening our long-term capabilities in the field of nuclear energy. Friends, Whether it is energy security or self-reliance in technology, India's goal is not limited to fulfilling its own needs. Our objective is also to expand our strength in critical technologies. This year, India achieved new milestones in the space sector as well. An Indian space startup successfully placed a satellite into orbit using a Made-in-India rocket in its very first attempt. India also achieved the capability of satellite docking. Today, many Indian startups are bringing new solutions in space science. The participation of young scientists and entrepreneurs in the space sector is also increasing significantly. Friends, The range of achievements India is making across various sectors is unprecedented. This very year, India hosted the AI Impact Summit and launched its own AI model. The first shipments of chips manufactured in India have now started reaching global markets. In the field of battery storage as well, we are working on capacity building based on raw materials available within India itself. Friends, From nuclear energy to space, and from AI to semiconductors, the requirements of these sectors are different. But their message is one and the same. India is strengthening both its capabilities and its confidence in emerging technologies. India possesses another strength as well: solutions at scale. Whenever a solution succeeds in India, it creates pathways for solving the problems of many countries around the world. Our Digital Stack is a wonderful example of this. Our 5G technology is another example. India is moving towards developing secure solutions that can reach people on a large scale. Friends, You all know that one challenge in the world of Science and Technology has always been the tendency to see it as a weapon. India, however, believes that Science and Technology are instruments for the service of humanity. The true success of science comes when it moves from the laboratory into the lives of ordinary citizens and improves them. Lab to land, the success of this technology is not determined merely by how advanced it is. Its real strength lies in how large a problem it solves and how many lives it transforms. Therefore, for us, Emerging Science and Technology means solutions that strengthen healthcare, transform agriculture, create new opportunities in education and employment, and help in disaster management. I am pleased that India's startup revolution is giving prominence to such questions. One startup is working on wearable EEG technology that helps understand brain activity. Indian companies are developing indigenous wireless and satellite communication solutions. In the field of nutrition, products are being developed keeping special dietary needs in mind. Indian companies are working on new technologies in the semiconductor sector. Solutions for utilising industrial waste such as steel slag are also being explored in our country. Friends, Just a few days ago, I had the opportunity to visit Chennai. I would like to share that experience with you as well. There, I inaugurated a modern Brain Centre at IIT Madras. This centre has developed a brain imaging technology platform that is a model for the entire world. This Brain Centre is working on developing high-resolution digital 3D images of the human brain. The success of this mission will help solve many complexities associated with neurological diseases. Science for Society, Technology for Transformation, this should be the benchmark of our scientific progress. And India is moving forward while measuring itself against this benchmark. Friends, We all know that a country's technology and technology-creation capability depends on its research. Research is the power that enables a nation to find solutions to its own problems and become self-reliant in critical technologies. That is why I have said: Jai Vigyan, Jai Anusandhan! For a Developed India, we are continuously strengthening our research sector. With this resolve, the Anusandhan National Research Foundation is being accelerated. Last year, on this very platform, we pledged an allocation of one lakh crore rupees for Research, Development, and Innovation. Today, the results are becoming visible. In the past year, nearly twelve thousand crore rupees have already been committed to technologies that are ready to move from the laboratory to the market. And private investors are participating equally in this effort. Alongside the government's commitment of twelve thousand crore rupees, the private sector is also investing twelve thousand crore rupees. This means that within just one year, a total new commitment of twenty-four thousand crore rupees has been made towards Research and Development. Friends, India's research ecosystem is also continuously expanding. Patent applications have seen substantial growth. Ten years ago, fewer than fifty thousand patent applications were filed in an entire year. Last year, this number rose to nearly one hundred and fifty thousand. These figures do not merely indicate rising numbers. They signify a transformation in which research and innovation are emerging as a strong culture within India. Friends, In this second quarter of the 21st century, another question has become extremely important. In the age of AI, how will scientific judgement evolve? The scientists leading our laboratories have developed their expertise through years of experience. On this stage too, several distinguished scientists and Nobel laureates are present. We all know that they developed their understanding by solving small problems, conducting experiments, and learning from failures. A scientist does not merely repeat available information. A scientist questions established patterns. A scientist examines solutions from new perspectives. A scientist identifies aspects that others may have overlooked. It is through this process that scientific judgement develops. Today, however, in the age of AI, a new challenge is emerging before us. AI can complete many routine tasks very quickly. This can improve research productivity. But we must also ensure that AI does not limit scientific thinking and independent judgement, but instead enriches them. The time saved through AI should be used for addressing more challenging problems, conducting new experiments, and testing new ideas. AI can provide answers quickly. But the responsibility of verifying the truth of those answers must remain with the scientist. Friends, We must continue preparing our scientists and institutions not only for today's needs but also for future challenges. Semiconductors, Quantum Technologies, Biotechnology, Advanced Materials, Robotics, Space, Clean Energy, and Next-Generation Communications are areas that will shape the future. India is building strong scientific foundations in all these fields. For this purpose, several National Missions have also been launched. Our objective is that new breakthroughs in these domains should emerge from India as well. And our responsibility extends beyond innovation alone. Whether it is AI, synthetic biology, or autonomous systems, safety and security must be given priority. In the age of AI, new challenges and new threats are also emerging in cybersecurity. From our financial networks to our national security systems, everyone faces this challenge. Therefore, it is essential that we work on AI-based solutions and advance quantum security solutions that can secure the country's systems against these threats. Our goal is to develop technologies that strengthen India while also contributing solutions to the challenges facing humanity. Friends, I appeal to every researcher, industry leader, startup founder, and young student present at this conclave to set a concrete goal for themselves. In the next three years, what technology, prototype, pilot project, or product will they contribute towards developing? Let us create innovations that support the making of a Developed India. Let us create innovations that make India a centre point for global solutions. Therefore, it is my hope that this conclave does not remain limited to an exchange of ideas alone. It should become a platform for measurable outcomes. Friends, Today, India is the youngest country in the world. We have talent. We have the resolve to create something new. We must build a generation that does not merely participate in Emerging Science and Technology, but leads it. Our spirit should be: We will do it. We will do it. Our confidence should be: We will do it. Once again, I extend my very best wishes for the success of this conclave. Thank you!

If any sport requires training abroad, the government is facilitating it and providing financial assistance. Governor of Maharashtra Shri Jishnu Dev Varma ji, Chief Minister Shri Devendra Fadnavis ji, my colleague in the Union Cabinet Shri Manohar Lal ji, Maharashtra Deputy Chief Minister Shri Eknath Shinde ji, Smt. Sunetra Ajit Pawar ji, all ministers, Members of Parliament and Members of the Legislative Assembly, other public representatives, and my dear brothers and sisters of Pune! Diwali is just around the corner. First of all, I extend my heartfelt greetings to all of you on the occasion of Diwali! Friends, The joy of festivals becomes even greater when a new facility is added to people's lives. Today is such a happy occasion for the people of Pune. I am pleased that another line of the Pune Metro is being inaugurated today. Ten years ago, I had the opportunity to lay the foundation stone for the first phase of this project in Pune. With the metro now starting between Hinjawadi and Shivajinagar, one of Pune's major IT hubs will receive improved connectivity. The new line whose work has commenced today will further strengthen connectivity between the old and new parts of Pune. I extend my heartfelt congratulations to all the people of Pune and Maharashtra. Friends, Pune has always been a centre of ideas that have given new direction to the nation and to society. Today, Pune is also providing direction to the new energy of a Viksit Bharat. From IT, automobiles, pharmaceuticals, startups, innovation, and manufacturing, Pune is a place of dreams and aspirations for the youth of the country. In such a situation, the strengthening metro connectivity here is set to give a new momentum to the development of Pune city. Friends, The inauguration and expansion of the Pune Metro demonstrates how we are building cities for a Viksit Bharat. Earlier, a city's identity was defined by its industries, or by its famous cuisine, fruits, or crops. But today, two more benchmarks have been added to the identity of cities. First, how competitive a city is. Second, the level of ease of living it offers. Metro projects help improve both these parameters for any city. As Pune Metro expands, it is saving time and providing greater convenience to the residents of Pune. And we all know that when a city's time is saved, its productivity increases. When a city's productivity increases, the country's economy gains momentum. And when the economy grows, both the poor and the middle class benefit. Friends, Today is also an example of pro-people, proactive good governance. The first metro in our country began operating in 1984. In the thirty years that followed, only 248 kilometres of metro lines were built. Two hundred and forty-eight kilometres. Until 2014, metro services existed in only five cities of the country. Had the same old thinking continued, had the old government remained in place, had the old work culture continued, it would have taken many more decades for the metro to reach Pune. But when the people gave the NDA an opportunity to serve in 2014, we moved forward with a resolve. That resolve was to improve ease of living and ease of transportation for the middle class living in our cities. Today, we can see the results. Over the past twelve years, metro transportation in the country has undergone a complete transformation. From 248 kilometres of metro routes in 2014, the network has now expanded to nearly 1,200 kilometres. From only five cities having metro services in 2014, metro systems are now operating in 26 cities. More than one crore people are now travelling by metro every day. Today, India's metro network is counted among the top three metro networks in the world. Friends, The situation in Maharashtra was similar to that in the rest of the country. When the NDA's double-engine government was formed for the first time in 2014, Maharashtra had only around 10-11 kilometres of operational metro lines. Today, nearly 175 kilometres of metro lines are in service in Maharashtra. Work is underway on more than 680 kilometres of metro corridors. Today, investments of more than ₹2.5 lakh crore are being made in Maharashtra's metro infrastructure alone. This money is going into the pockets of workers. This money is generating employment for engineers. This money is creating jobs in shops and factories. Friends, This metro project is special for another reason as well. This metro line has been constructed on a PPP model. That is, it has been built through the joint efforts of the government and the private sector. This is a development model whose success will further accelerate the country's progress. Friends, Today, every citizen of India, every young son and daughter of India, is working with full strength to build a Viksit Bharat. Today everyone is confidently saying: We Will Do It. We Will Do It. This spirit exists throughout the country. Over the past twelve years, the nation's energy has been reflected in village roads, highways, expressways, electrification of new railway lines, long tunnels, and towering bridges. Numerous projects and schemes are taking shape across the country. In the coming years, along with this, the country will devote its energy to making its cities among the best in the world. Friends, Our cities are not merely places to live. They are also major centres of economic activity. We must transform hundreds of small cities across the country into growth hubs. It was with this vision that the Urban Challenge Fund was approved in February this year. The central government is providing support of ₹1 lakh crore for this fund. The goal is to attract investments worth many lakh crores of rupees into cities across the country in the coming years. I urge local bodies, our friends in the private sector, and everyone else to come forward. We must all work in mission mode. I am confident that Maharashtra will expand its role further in this effort. Friends, There are two additional reasons why India is being discussed throughout the world today. First, India has maintained the fastest growth rate in the world despite difficult global circumstances. Second, within just ten to twelve years, fifty crore people in India have overcome extreme poverty. This fact has emerged in the latest World Bank report. Many generations in the country grew up hearing promises and claims about eradicating poverty. Earlier, the fight against poverty existed only in slogans. Today, India is defeating poverty on the ground. Friends, Today, India is the country that is both the fastest-growing economy and the fastest reducer of poverty. These two achievements are interconnected. India's economy is growing rapidly. India's budget is expanding. Investments in the people are increasing. The money that earlier disappeared through scams, favoritism, and leakages is now reaching beneficiaries directly. When the nation's resources are invested in roads, railways, airports, and metro systems, when the government increases investment in electricity, water, and gas pipelines, people's lives improve and employment opportunities increase. Friends, Facilities such as homes for the poor, toilets, tap water for every household, and gas connections have increased people's confidence and saved their time. And this saved time is being used to earn additional income for their families. Friends, Just last month, with your blessings, I completed twenty-five years as the head of government. The motto of our government has always been that when you empower the poor, the poor gain the courage to lift themselves out of poverty. I remember that when I was Chief Minister of Gujarat, I started the tradition of Garib Kalyan Melas with this very thought. These events focused on delivering welfare schemes directly to the poor. Poor citizens were invited onto the stage. They would take a pledge to defeat poverty and return home with renewed confidence. Friends, The work that I began twenty-five years ago continues uninterrupted even today. This is what I call Power to Empower. Power to Empower is true good governance. This transformation did not happen overnight. Previous governments grew tired of merely raising slogans. We connected every link. We wove together the dreams of millions of lives. This is the result of years of thoughtful and well-planned efforts. And that is why fifty crore Indians have defeated poverty. Friends, When poverty declines, the benefits are not limited to those families that escape it. It injects new energy into the country's economy. It creates a new middle class. This neo-middle class brings new dreams. Its purchasing power drives markets. New opportunities emerge, from shops to factories. In other words, the reduction of poverty in India is taking the Indian economy to new heights. Friends, In this journey towards a Viksit Bharat, our farmer brothers and sisters also have a major role. Therefore, our government is continuously empowering farmers. However, due to adverse weather conditions in Maharashtra, many farming families are facing difficulties at present. We must not forget the difficulties faced by our drought-affected citizens, our farmers, and others living in villages. Together, we will face this challenge. Once again, my best wishes to all of you for these metro projects. My heartfelt greetings for the upcoming festivals. Thank you very much. People of Pune, repeat after me with full strength, raising both your hands: Bharat Mata Ki Jai! Bharat Mata Ki Jai! Bharat Mata Ki Jai!
Greetings to all of you! This is the festive season; the nation is filled with celebration, enthusiasm, and energy. In this atmosphere of joy and celebration, I welcome and greet all of you friends who have come here from all over the world. Friends, This NDTV summit is dedicated to deliberating on serious matters. Nowadays, everyone is talking about a new world order. Because this affects ordinary life, the importance of such world summits increases even more. Friends When talking about 'The World Between Orders', we need to understand two things. First, why is the world order changing? Why is the old global order unable to provide solutions to today's challenges? And second, what is India's role in this changing world? Friends, If I elaborate on the first point, this change is natural and spontaneous. The Second World War taught the world a major lesson. The world realized that a global order cannot function merely on the basis of power and self-interest. Dialogue is essential, cooperation is necessary, and resolving disputes is equally vital. It was this sentiment that laid the foundation of the United Nations, or the UN. For decades, the UN played a significant role in promoting global cooperation and peace. But with time, the world kept changing. New aspirations emerged. New economies and new powers arose. The challenges confronting the world also became far more complex than ever before. Technology transformed society, the economy, and the very nature of power. And countries that were not at the table when the global order was initially established came forward with their own prominent roles. The question then arises: did the global order change at the same pace? The answer is no. Neither did the world order change, nor could global institutions adapt themselves with equivalent speed. The United Nations and other global institutions have had their historic role. Yet in the face of today's challenges, their limitations are clearly visible. Now, emerging powers are rising. New voices are demanding their rightful place. New challenges demand new solutions. When the reality of the world shifts, but decision-making methods remain unchanged, a gap begins to widen between the system and reality. That is precisely what we are witnessing today. For decades, trade connected nations, technology closed distances, maritime routes linked economies, and culture and mobility brought diverse societies closer together; but today, these very links face fresh strains. Trade is being weaponized. Technology is turning into a new arena of competition and conflict. Sea routes are emerging as epicenters of geopolitical tension. Therefore, the challenge today is not merely about the emergence of a new global order. The real challenge is: in a changing world, how do we build an order where new realities find their place, global institutions become more effective, and the spirit of cooperation among nations is reinforced? And this is where the question of India's role comes to the fore. Friends, Before elaborating on India's role, I will take you back by 7 to 8 years. What transpired during the COVID pandemic is something we all witnessed. When the world needed cooperation the most, it appeared completely divided. Those who made grand proclamations about globalization prioritized their own self-interests in a time of crisis. In that era of fragmentation, everyone viewed each other with suspicion, and global trust had hit rock bottom; it was then, in 2020, from the G20 platform, that I placed an idea before the world, calling for "a new world order for the collective well-being of humankind" - an order with humanity at its very core. That same year, from the UNGA platform, I said, "If the century changes and we do not, our ability to bring about change also weakens." Friends, Today, while it is true that the world order is changing, the real question is: as the old global order shifts, what kind of new world order do we want to build together? Reforms in multilateral institutions are essential. It is vital to grant the Global South its rightful place and voice. International cooperation must not be reserved only for good times; it must remain effective during crises, and for this, meaningful consensus among all stakeholders is imperative. Friends, In our heritage, Chanakya stated in the Arthashastra: 'Apranitah tu matsya-nyayam ud-bhavayati' - meaning, when governance weakens, the "law of the fish" (matsya nyaya) takes over. Under matsya nyaya, the big fish swallows the smaller fish. The world needs a system that prevents such a situation from arising. That is India's vision, and that is precisely what India is doing. The world experienced this role of India during the severe crisis of the pandemic. When capable nations were thinking solely of themselves, India stepped forward, delivered vaccines to every needy nation, and dispatched medicines to over 100 countries. Friends, Guided by the principle of 'Sabka Saath, Sabka Vikas', India is performing its duty as a Vishwa-Bandhu (universal friend). You have witnessed this firsthand: the G20 Summit was held under India's presidency, and the BRICS Summit was held under India's leadership. How many doubts were voiced regarding both summits? It was said that building consensus on joint declarations would be impossible. But India built bridges, emphasized dialogue, and on both occasions, the Delhi Declaration was adopted through unanimous consensus. Nations considered bitter rivals came together on India's platform. This is not mere coincidence. This is the trust placed in India's role as a Vishwa-Bandhu. Friends, The stronger and more empowered India becomes, the greater the benefit to humanity. This is because India's policies and decisions are inspired by timeless experience and civilizational values. India does not make decisions driven by fleeting moments. Hence, India is a guarantee of stability and an assurance of continuity. Our resolve for a Viksit Bharat (Developed India) also fosters this very spirit. Just a few days ago at a summit, I presented the vision of "Power to Empower." A developed India is not just for ourselves; it is a guarantee for a better world. When India advances toward self-reliance, it strengthens global supply chains. When India's economy grows stronger, it provides hope of prosperity to the world. When India's youth become capable and skilled, it inspires global confidence in innovation and productivity. In other words, India is enhancing its own capabilities today so that the world's capacity can also increase. Friends, India is working with all its might to become a top-3 economy. Our confidence grows even further when we look at the present difficult global circumstances, in the midst of which India continues to emerge as the fastest-growing major economy. Today, India is among the top economies in green energy. India ranks among the top three AI ecosystems. India is among the top three startup ecosystems. India is one of the largest digital economies. India stands number one in real-time digital payments. Friends, Today's India is expanding its capabilities across various sectors, and there are numerous milestones achieved for the first time in the country. India's Fast Breeder Reactor achieved criticality for the first time. India linked the Census with digital technology for the first time. During this period, India's first hydrogen train began operations, and India crossed the 300-gigawatt mark in non-fossil electricity capacity for the first time. Friends, I will give you another example. A few days ago, I had the opportunity to meet young athletes returning from the Asian Games. India won 85 medals this time, achieving many milestones for the first time. The women's recurve archery team won team and individual gold medals for the first time. In boxing, India won three gold medals in a single Asian Games, which is also a first. Friends, Now I will mention a term, and I hope many people in the hall have heard it. The word is - Sepak Takraw. Sepak Takraw, have you heard this word? For those who do not know, let me tell you that it is the name of a sport. The world plays it and has been winning medals in it. It is essentially a hybrid combination of football and volleyball. It is played with the feet, but the action takes place across a net. India won a medal for the first time in recent Asian Games in an event of this sport - the Men's Quadrant. Friends, This is the hallmark of a changing India. In every sector, something new and unprecedented is taking place. That means India's conventional strengths are being reinforced, and where we were once not as strong, we are emerging as a new force. None of this is happening in isolation; it is the direct outcome of the policy of 'Reform, Perform, Transform'. Friends, The country has seen days when the entire power grid would collapse under rising electricity demand. But when a nation rises with sheer willpower and sets forth, the power grid does not collapse; instead, that very grid fuels the nation's progress and soaring confidence. Friends, Today, our youth power, women power, our farmers, fishermen, the poor, and the middle class are engaged with high spirits in the building of a Viksit Bharat. Look around today: India's exports are setting new records. Speaking of September alone, exports of goods and products increased by nearly 25 percent compared to last year. Seafood exports have more than doubled in the last 12 years. In our villages, more than 3.5 crore women have become Lakhpati Didis, and we are working toward the goal of creating 6 crore Lakhpati Didis. Under the Pradhan Mantri Viksit Bharat Rozgar Yojana, around 70 lakh new jobs have been created in a very short span of time. For the middle class, income tax has been made zero up to 12 lakh rupees - this needs to be reminded to you again and again; it cannot be that you simply take what you get and forget it. Ease of Living is improving across the country, and new employment opportunities are being generated. Friends, There was a time when the world referred to India as the 'World's Back Office', but today's India is not merely a service provider; we are innovating, designing, and creating new solutions and technologies for the world. Look at the field of research alone: today we rank third in research publications. In the year 2025, nearly 1.5 lakh patents were filed - 1.5 lakh patents in a single year. This offers a glimpse into the immense potential for research and innovation in India. Friends, Consider the semiconductor sector as well: nearly 20 percent of the world's chip-design workforce is in India. Indian engineers within the country are working on advanced 2 to 3-nanometer chip designs. To expand this talent pool further, every necessary facility is being provided in the country today. In Indian academic institutions, our students have designed more than 325 chips so far. Friends, Another example is Global Capability Centres. More than half of the world's capability centres are located in India. It is not just about the numbers; the nature of their work is changing. Previously, many centres focused solely on support services. Today, they operate across fields like AI, Cloud, Engineering, Analytics, and Product Development. In other words, India's role is shifting, and India's identity is evolving. We have moved from 'Talent at Scale' to 'Thinking at Scale'. The world that once trusted India's manpower now places equal trust in India's mind-power. As a result, we are working alongside the world not merely as a Back Office, but as a Brain Office. Friends, Whenever the government frames a policy or takes an investment decision in any sector, there is clear thinking behind it: what capacities India needs to develop, in which domains we must reduce dependence on other nations, and which impending crises we must prepare for today. We keep these factors in mind with every decision. There are many investments whose benefits take years to become visible, but they hold immense significance for the nation's future. Let me share an interesting example. All of you are familiar with India's pharmaceutical capabilities, but until a few years ago, India relied heavily on imports for raw materials and medicines. Now the country is changing this situation. You must have heard of Penicillin G, which is essential for antibiotics. Over the last two to three decades, its domestic production in India had practically ceased. We brought it under the Production Linked Incentive (PLI) Scheme. Today, after many years, domestic production of Penicillin G has resumed in Kakinada, Andhra Pradesh. Friends, Penicillin G is just one example. In India, we are building an entire domestic supply chain for APIs (Active Pharmaceutical Ingredients) - the fundamental raw materials for medicine manufacturing. That is why this sector was brought under the PLI scheme, attracting over 5,000 crore rupees in investment so far, with around 40 projects already underway. Such policies and decisions are steering India toward self-reliance. Friends, Amidst discussions of so many national achievements, our media friends might complain that while much was said, nothing was spoken about their work. NDTV is hosting such a magnificent event, and our motto is 'Sabka Saath, Sabka Vikas' - which includes the development of the media - so perhaps you would appreciate a few candid and sweet-and-sour remarks as well? Friends, While the world is searching for a new order, some people are dreaming of disorder in India. Today, when there is so much disarray in the world, the image of a strong India should be conveyed globally. Yet these individuals are bent on tarnishing India's reputation. They travel abroad to defame India and plead for foreign intervention. One wonders how long Congress will continue to wager the nation's interests just to launch its failed product. In an entire year, Sriharikota does not attempt as many launches as Congress makes failed attempts to launch its leader. Friends, Whatever decline Congress chooses to bring upon itself is entirely its own concern, and we have no problem with that. But to protect one family from the repeated stain of failure, Congress is putting India's achievements, strengths, and progress on trial. The grapes are so sour that Congress has set about cutting down the grapevine itself. In reality, this family has become a burden on their party; senior elders of the party understand this and even share it with us in private, yet they swallow their humiliation and remain silent. Friends, This conduct of Congress harms honest critics of the government as well, and this imposes a huge cost in a democracy. Honest critics suffer immensely because of this. Those who wish to present their critique against the government in a democratic manner find their voices drowned out by Congress's clamour; the voices of genuine critics are suppressed. Those who genuinely care for democracy and the national interest - even if they are staunch opponents of the government - find no opportunity to speak amidst the shouting and screeching of Congress. We have seen in Parliament: there are so many talented young Members of Parliament from opposition parties who wish to put forward their views and critique the government, but Congress disrupts Parliament so that others do not get an opportunity, simply because Congress wants to keep only one person in the limelight. By doing so, Congress does a grave injustice to the young MPs of the opposition. And friends, Movements that organically aim to highlight the government's shortcomings and genuinely seek systemic reform - which is indeed necessary in a democracy - are also ruined by Congress. Congress has become a parasite that does not want anyone else to progress. Intellectual Naxals have captured Congress's mind to such an extent that Congress has lost its own capacity to think. Those who once wrought destruction in forests have infiltrated Congress and are now bent on wreaking havoc on the country. Friends, Amidst all these challenges, the grand goal of a Viksit Bharat stands before us. The nation's aspirations are boundless. Therefore, a single spirit resonates across India today, and that spirit is: 'We Will Do It, We Will Do It.' We will dream big, turn every challenge into an opportunity, and translate every resolution into reality. Our strength is our self-confidence; our inspiration lies in the dreams of 140 crore fellow citizens. We will innovate, forge new paths, reach new heights, and link India's success with the world's success. I am confident that the nectar emerging from this summit will empower this very spirit, infuse our resolutions with new energy, and play a vital role in realizing India's grand aspirations. Once again, my warmest wishes to all of you. Thank you very much.
My colleagues in the Union Cabinet Shri Prahlad Joshi ji, DU Vice Chancellor Yogesh Singh ji, SRCC Principal Simrit Kaur ji, Ajay ji, Mallika ji, Rajat ji, all companions connected with SRCC, faculty, staff, SRCC alumni, and my young friends! I am the sixth player. Today's occasion is very historic for Shri Ram College of Commerce, and equally important for the country's education world. Here, many generations who have been part of SRCC have gathered together. I extend my heartfelt congratulations to all of you for this centenary celebration of SRCC. I respectfully pay tribute to its founder, Sir Shri Ram ji, and bow to his vision. Friends, When a person lives for 100 years, he is considered an ocean of experiences. And when an institution completes 100 years, it becomes an ocean of achievements and inspirations. In its 100‐year journey, SRCC has shaped generations, connecting them to a bright future. Those who came studied, and those who left went out as well‐shaped individuals. The journey of this college began in Daryaganj, in a small place, and today that small college has become a huge tree. A tree that has given shade to many generations, and enriched them with its coolness. Even today, in DU, when someone asks which college, saying SRCC itself is a flex in itself. Friends, The alumni of SRCC have also been very special. If I say in your language, the alumni here are the OGs, who carried SRCC's name across the world, making their mark in different fields. Rajat ji mentioned a long list. And I too have had the opportunity to work with some of them. Our Arun Jaitley ji studied here. And I can say that perhaps there was never a meeting with him where he did not narrate some incident of SRCC. Sometimes I would get tired, because he would repeat the same incident again and again. In my team today, brother Sanjeev Sanyal also studied here. Many other companions are here too. And it is not that only people from the world of economics came out of here - many artists, leaders, lawyers, judges have also come from here. That is, SRCC people have created a stir in every field. They may not all be giants, but they certainly created a stir. Friends, For the past few days, this program has been much discussed. Especially people are remembering my 2013 address. People have listened to it again, read it again. It is possible that the new generation has also revisited it. That speech, I can say, became a wave, a movement. And even today, 13-14 years later, the impact of that gathering, that address, remains. Friends, Then, in your place, your seniors were sitting. And I was then a Chief Minister, but also a member of the country's main opposition party. And perhaps people then expected that I would make long attacks on the central government of that time, shower accusations. But friends, Even while being in opposition, even while being a critic of the then government, I did not use that platform for that. I placed a vision, spoke positive things. Whatever I said then was my conviction. I have always lived those words, those dreams. I have always walked that path - dedicating myself wholeheartedly for the country, living with the spirit of serving the nation. That inner world of mine, that emotional universe, on that day in 2013, from this very platform, came out one by one in a garland of words. And I spoke at length. Then I asked the students if I should continue, and they loudly said, "Yes, continue." Friends, That day I gave the example of a glass of water, and it was much discussed in the country. I explained the three perspectives of looking at a glass half‐filled with water. One, those who see the glass as half full of water. Second, those who see the glass as half empty. And third, those who see the glass as completely full - half with water and half with air. Friends, This was the time when the country was sunk in the swamp of despair. Even then I said that I do not see the glass as half, I do not see it as incomplete - I see the glass as completely full. Half with water, half with air. And at that time you all must have been in school, but today if you go on the internet and read the news of 2013 and around that period, you will realize what the situation was. And I urge the youth - just look once at that period of 2013 on the internet, the headlines around my speech then. Whether newspapers or TV channels, what were the headlines then? Let me give you a glimpse of those headlines. Now there is a gang that will cut my words into small pieces and put them in my account, so I warn beforehand that what I am telling you is what dominated the media in 2013, such as: • Indian soldiers killed on the LoC - today's generation may find it strange that such things happened, but they did. • World Bank lowered GDP growth forecast. • Current account deficit at an all‐time high. • Inflation rate around ten percent. • Industrial sector caught in recession. • Helicopter deal bribery exposed. • Bomb blasts in Hyderabad. These were the headlines of that time. I am sure today's students do not know all this. These things show what the condition of the country was then. Growth was on the floor and inflation was on the ceiling. Scams were at their peak and the corrupt roamed fearlessly. The economy was in bad shape, everywhere there was talk of policy paralysis. The world counted India among the "Fragile Five." Terrorists were unchecked and Pakistan was uncontrollable. That is, then there was nothing but despair and frustration. Friends, That was the day when on every front the country was struggling, and this is today - today those spreading terrorism are subdued. If Pakistan does any nefarious act, India's forces enter their home and strike. From Jammu & Kashmir to the North East, peace has increased. Maoist terror, Naxalism has been broken. The world that was then worried about policy paralysis in India, today talks about India's policy dynamism. Then the world called India part of the Fragile Five, today the world sees India becoming the fastest‐growing major economy. Just in the past week, many positive news items have come about India's economy. The 7.8 percent quarterly growth has boosted the nation's enthusiasm, and in the same period a Japanese credit rating agency has upgraded India's rating. And friends, This 7.8 percent growth has come at a time when war is going on in West Asia, when there have been many trade‐related obstacles. To achieve such growth at such a time shows India's capability in itself. Friends, I know, you also know, those who were infamous for pushing India into the Fragile Five, they cannot digest these figures. But you all understand the numbers seriously. Between April and June, electricity production increased by 9 percent, vehicle sales grew by more than 20 percent. In that too, two‐wheelers grew by 20 percent, and three‐wheelers by nearly 30 percent. That means consumer demand is running, and commercial activity is strong. Growth in steel and cement consumption increased by 8 percent. That means construction, housing, infrastructure, and industrial activity are all rising. That means India is growing, and creating new employment opportunities too. Despite this, for those who saw the glass half empty in 2013, their thinking remains the same even today. For them the glass will always be half empty. When I speak of the glass, I am not speaking of anything being empty. Friends, In 2013, speaking here at SRCC, I also spoke of P2G2 - Pro‐People Good Governance. Those who ran governments for long had a style - wake up only when there is fire, dig a well only when there is drought. I spoke of changing this approach. And after forming government in 2014, we have shown this in action. Let me give you an example. Friends, Recently, the Jan Dhan Yojana completed 12 years. Today you are living in a completely different era of fintech, but those were also the days of 2013 when students from villages or small towns had to struggle a lot even to open savings bank accounts. At that time our population had crossed 1.25 billion, but by March 2014, more than 50 percent of the country's population did not even have bank accounts. And those outside the banking system were mostly the poor, women, migrant laborers, students. That is why we brought the Jan Dhan scheme, and in the past 12 years, because of Jan Dhan Yojana, nearly 600 million new bank accounts have been opened. Today almost every family in the country is connected to the banking system. This became the foundation of India's fintech revolution. Friends, Now think also about those people who opposed even connecting millions of people to the banking system. In fact, such people enjoyed only when the glass was half full, so that the poor ordinary citizens kept circling around them. This "mai‐baap" culture they had continued for decades. But this is Modi - standing like a rock before those who liked the mai‐baap culture. Friends, There is an old saying: truth fears no fire. I have courage, and that is why I am reminding you of what I said that day, bringing my report card before you. I know that before the roar of truth, the echo of lies cannot stand. Friends, In that 2013 program itself, when I said that we must work on Made in India, the people of the old ecosystem of that time could not even understand it. They thought that in that era of despair, Made in India would not be possible at all! And those who grew up in that thinking still mock Made in India. But friends, Today you are seeing the brand value of Make in India. Let me explain with an example. Today every hand has a smartphone. The world's biggest phone brands are making India their base. Made in India smartphones are reaching every corner of the world. In 2014, India imported most of its mobile phones. Today India exports mobile phones worth more than 2.5 lakh crore rupees. Friends, I am not a person who takes small and easy targets. I set big goals for the country, and I put my whole life into achieving them. Look at the defense sector. Earlier, most things for India's armed forces were bought from abroad. As soon as I came, I told the defense people - make a negative list. First they made a list of 500 items, saying these we will not bring from outside. Then they made 1,000, then 3,000. Army, Navy, Air Force - many times even for a small piece of equipment they had to wait for months. We made a plan together with the armed forces, made lists of items, then told Indian companies to make all these. And today you see, the country's defense manufacturing is at its all‐time high. Today India is producing defense equipment worth nearly 2 lakh crore rupees. In 2013-14, we exported defense equipment worth 600-700 crore rupees. Today this figure has reached nearly 40,000 crore rupees. And these achievements are possible because today a vibrant defense manufacturing ecosystem is being built in the country. Friends, I have trust in the country, trust in the country's capability, trust in you youth. And on the basis of this trust I speak of a developed India. I am confident that we 1.4 billion Indians together will make our country developed and self‐reliant. • In the coming time, India will be a hub of manufacturing. • In the coming time, Hindustan will be a hub of research and innovation. • India will be a superpower of agricultural exports. My young friends, • India will have its own space station. And you will also see the day when India will host an Olympics in such a way that the world will be left watching in awe. And these are not just words, these are our resolutions. And to turn these resolutions into reality, continuous work is happening in the country. Friends, Right now your life is running in SRCC mode. Whether it is tea at the co‐op, discussions over cold coffee, last‐minute notes for exams, rushing for printouts, or the hard work put into Crossroads and the Business Conclave - you have made every moment here a memory of your life. But outside this campus, a new world awaits you. You will meet two kinds of people. One, those who positively transform the power of society into the power of the nation. The second species are those who, whenever any work begins, become the "angry uncle." Their favorite job is to oppose everything. Their permanent dialogue is: "What is the need for this? What is the need for this?" That is the permanent line of every such uncle. When we were building the world's tallest statue, these uncles tightened their belts and said, "What is the need for this?" When we started work on the high‐speed train, they again said, "What is the need for this?" When we brought UPI, these same people said, "Why UPI? What is the need for UPI?" When we spoke of making chips, again they said, "What is the need for this?" When we built the new Parliament building, they said, "What is the need for this?" When we built a memorial for our brave military commanders who gave the supreme sacrifice for the nation, again they came out saying, "What was the need for this?" But India has answered all such uncles - whatever is necessary for the country, no one can stop India from doing it. Friends, Today India is moving forward with a different temperament. Work that earlier took decades is now happening within just a few years. Let me give you another example with full facts. India's first electric train ran in 1925. Your college started in 1926, but the train ran in 1925. After that, until 2014 - that is, 90 years - less than 30 percent of railway lines were electrified. In just 12 years, we have shown nearly 100 percent electrification of railway lines. Friends, This speed in India's policy decisions is being seen and understood by the rest of the world. That is why they are increasing their partnerships with India. In the past decade, trade agreements and free trade agreements have been signed with nearly 40 countries. Because of this, new markets are opening for leather, textiles, fruits and vegetables, our processed food, shrimp, and many other products. Who better than you knows what it means when new markets open, when tariffs are reduced for India's goods and services. This is creating a new sky of opportunities for India's youth. Friends, Today, compared to 2013, the country is far more secure economically and socially. But seeing this, some people's restlessness and frustration are increasing. You must have heard my speech on 15th August. For such people, I gave a dose of homeopathy from the Red Fort - a small homeopathy pill. And you know, they say the nature of homeopathy is that when you start the medicine, at first the illness flares up even more. That is the nature of homeopathy. So from the Red Fort I gave a homeopathy pill - "mental Naxal." This mental Naxal treatment, as I said, first flares up the illness for some time, and then begins the cure. In the same way, as soon as I gave the mental Naxal pill, all the mental Naxals started coming out. And those who are promoting this illness, now the public is seeing their true colors too. Friends, I have said so many things, some people may think - oh, what is the big deal, it was a country of 1.4 billion, it was easy, what new did Modi do? But was it really so easy? Let me take you back once again to the old days. Remember when you were in school. In 2013, when the disaster struck Kedarnath, the entire government was shaken. They did not know what to do, how to do it. In 2008, when the banking crisis came, the government of that time tried to hide behind that crisis until 2014, not daring to even touch it. India's entire banking system was brought to the brink of collapse. In 2008 itself, when the horrific terrorist attack happened in Mumbai, the government then came under external pressure, but did not even show the courage to teach Pakistan a lesson. Friends, In the past 12 years, many disasters have struck the country, we have faced huge cyclones, but the nation has faced them strongly. In the past 6 years, very big crises have come. First the pandemic of corona, which shut down the whole world. Then the war broke out between Russia and Ukraine. After that, the era began of weaponizing trade and supply chains. Then came the crisis in West Asia. Every time it was said, now India will not survive. And those who are my "special admirers" were happy thinking - now Modi is trapped, now Modi will fall, now he will be crushed. But the blessings of my country's people have such strength, such strength, that every wish of those who wanted India's downfall has been left unfulfilled. Friends, Now in the 21st century, a world of new opportunities is before us. I am happy that you are ambitious, because ambition cannot be taught from a textbook. Ambition comes from the environment that inspires you to dream big. Today our young companions say - if I do something, I will start a startup, if I build a company, I will make it a unicorn. I will join sectors like space and drones, I will do something big. I will build cutting‐edge AI solutions, I will innovate in agriculture. Thirteen years ago, your seniors could not even dream such dreams. But today you are dreaming them, because in today's India an ecosystem has been built to support those dreams. Friends, Now is the time to set big goals and achieve them. Just recently from the Red Fort I said some things. I want to repeat them among you. Take a pledge - why can't India have global consulting firms? Why can't our accounting firms, our legal firms, our financial firms become global leaders? We proudly say that today Indians are leading the world's top companies. If Indians are leading the world's top companies, then why can't companies created by Indians lead the world? Friends, All this is possible. For this you have both networks and knowledge. Friends, Our inspiration, your inspiration, is also the SRCC college crest. It has two beautiful symbols. One is the rising sun, and the other is the ship moving forward in the sea. The sun, which shows direction, and the ship, which moves forward in that direction. We must walk on such new paths, where the sun is ours, the sea is ours. The boat is ours, the sailor is ours. The courage to write victory on the waves is ours. The flight is ours, the sky is ours. The spirit to face strong winds is ours. The dream is ours, the resolve is ours, the banner of self‐reliant India is ours. On the path of developed India, every step rising swiftly is ours. Once again, on this special occasion, I extend my many best wishes to all connected with SRCC. Vande Mataram.
